techniques and tips for building agents.
While you dont have to be a strong Python developer to enjoy the content in this book,
some famili arity will certainly help. You will also need access to an OpenAI API subscription
to work through the examples in the book. This will require purchasing LLM usage but
expect not to spend more than 20 dollars USD .
Since my AI agent journey began in 2023, I have been looking at productizing and
commercializing agents  for enterprise applica tions. During this time, I have gathered a
breadth of knowledge that filled the first edition and has since been further updated in the
second edition with more formal practical techniques and tips for building real-world agentic
systems.
A lot has changed with our understanding of agents and agentic systems. We have gone
from the single agent helper to now building full autonomous agentic workflows that enlist
multiple types of agents all working together . For this reason, this book focuses on
exploring single and multiple agent patterns from many practical use cases including
retriev al augmented gener ation, customer support and deep research agentic systems.
On top of those new agentic patterns, new protocols have also been introduced, such as
model context protocol (MCP). MCP has becom e an essential tool for the agent developer ,
and we will spend a significant amount of time consuming and building these serv er tools.
I look forward to all your feedback and questions. Feedback can help shape the book into
a great resource to help others following in your footsteps in building agents. Any
suggestions you provide in the liveBook discussion forum  will as always be greatly

The agent is not a new concept in machine learning and artificial intelligence. In
reinforcement learning,  for instance, the word agent  denotes active decisio n-making and
learning intelligence. In other areas, the word agent  aligns more with an automated
application or softw are that does something on your behalf . At its essence, the term agent
refers to an entit y that can act on behalf of another .
An AI agent has agency , as in the ability to make decisions, undertak e tasks, and act for
someone or something. This is in stark contrast to what we may refer to as the classic AI
assistant  or chatbot. ChatGPT and Claude are becoming more agentic, allowing you to
delegate work through tools or agents like Deep Research. This can all get a little muddled,
so we will review the boundaries between assistant and agent shortly .
This book focuses on agentshow they work, what makes them powerful, and how we
can build agents and agentic systems to automate complex tasks. While there are multiple
patterns for building AI Agents, this book will focus on building agents powered b y LLMS .
You will learn to build and connect these agents using modern frameworks like the
OpenAI Agents SDK and the Model Context  Protocol, linking them to databases, APIs,
knowledge stores, and complete applications.  The goal is clear: to guide you from prompt
tinkerer to agent archit ect, equipped with patterns, code, and mental models that let your
agents earn their k eep in production.1
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook

1.1 Defining agents and agentic thinking
There has long been some confusion on what makes an agent. To help clarify the term, lets
review the evolution from direct LLM action s, to assistant proxies, assistants and now
agents.
1.1.1 Understanding agent/assistant and LLM patterns
You likely already have direct experience chatting with an LLM such as ChatGPT or Claude
to get answers to questions, gener ate code, or learn. One step up from these interactions is
asking an LLM to use a tool to create an image, search the web, or carry out some other
task. Figure 1.1 shows  examples where a user may interact with an LLM directly (top) or
through an LLM that uses tools, called an assistant (bottom). Notice how using ChatGPT as
an assistant better formulates the prompt for image gener ation.
Figure 1.1 Common patterns for directly communicating with an LLM or an LLM with tools. If youve used
earlier versions of ChatGPT, you experienced direct interaction with the LLM. No proxy agent or other
assistant interjected on your behalf. Today, ChatGPT itself has plenty of tools it uses to help respond from web
search, coding and so on, making the current version function like an assistant.
If you have used Sora or other image gener ation tools through platforms like ChatGPT or
Gemini, then you have experienced a proxy agent interaction. In this case, an LLM
intercepts y our requests and reformulates them in a format better designed for the task.2
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 9 ---
From these two direct communication patterns, we have evolved into tool-using
assistants and agents. Here. we use the term assistant  to refer to an LLM that can act on
behalf of the user to use tools but lacks the agency to perform and complete multiple tasks.
In contrast, agents  are entities  with the agency to work autonomously to reason, plan, and
undertak e tasks such as send emails, create presentations, book flights and perform
research. Figure 1.2 shows a comparison of the assistant and agent patterns; as you can
see the key difference is that the assistant s tasks must be appro ved by the user, while the
agent can carry out tasks without human appro val.
Figure 1.2 Top: an assistant performs a single or multiple tasks on behalf of a user, where each task requires
approval by the user. Bottom: An agent may use multiple tools autonomously without human approval to
complete a goal,
In the figure, the assistant (top) uses a single tool to call the weather service, to complete
the task of answering a query about the weather , but first asks appro val from the user to
use the tool. Conversely the user gives the agent (bottom) the power to make decisions,
reason, and plan to use tools to complete a higher goal. Here, the user empowers the agent
to select the proper tools, email and notif y, to filter the emails.3
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 10 ---
if you have ever used a ChatGPT tool such as web search, canvas or GPT assistant, then
you have used an assistant. In this case, the LLM is aware of the tools or assistant
functions and will prepare to make calls to these functions. Howev er, before making a call
to a new external tool, the LLM will require a break and ask for user appro val. The tools are
executed if appro ved, and the results are returned to the LLM. The LLM will then
reformulate the response in natur al language and return it to the user .
In contrast, the agent interprets the user's request or goal and may then reason and
construct a plan to identif y further decision points. From this, it executes the steps in the
plan and makes the required decisions independently . It may request user feedback after
specific milestone tasks, but this agent has the power and ability to create and execute a
plan to complete a goal. Agency empowers agents to accomplish complex goals, but care
must be tak en to guard them from performing unknown actions.
1.1.2 Thinking like agents
We use the term agency to describe an agent's ability to make decisions and use tools to
complete a goal. Internally , this means that agents (LLMs) need to be able to reason
through the tasks to accomplish a goal. Table 1.1 provides an example of three goals,
creating an image, traveling to Calgary , and buying a computer , along with the tasks
performed to complete those goals and the tools used to carry out those tasks.
Table 1.1 Example goals and the tasks and tools needed to carry them out.
Goals Tasks Tools
Create Image Create image create_image
Travel to
Calgary (YYC)Search flights
Book flights
Book hotels
Book tr ansportationsearch_flights
book_flights
book_hotels
book_tr ansportation
Buy a computer Search for computers
Compare and match features/prices
Order computerweb_search
web_search
order
Goals may be as simple as creating an image or as complex as booking travel or buying
something. The tasks needed to complete a goal are broken down to the tool level, so each
task is the execution of a tool. Tools may be designed to be task specific, such as create
images or more gener al, such as a web search which could be used for multiple tasks.
Agents receiv e a goal from the user or system and then break down the goal into tasks.
From there the agent performs each of the tasks necessary to achiev e the goal. Figure 1.3
illustr ates an agent's typical thought process for taking a goal from a user and completing
tasks using tools; the process has four basic steps: sense, plan, act, and learn.4
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 11 ---
Figure 1.3 The four-step process agents use to complete goals: Sense (receive input  goal or feedback) ->
Plan (define the task list that completes the goal) -> Act (execute tool defined by task) -> Learn (observe the
output of the task and determine if goal is complete or process needs to continue) ->
The Sense, Plan, Act, Learn cycle is an internal mechanism agents use to complete goals
that require multiple task actions. Agency and the ability to decide and plan are what
powers this loop for agents.
When configuring an agent, we typically provide it with the necessary tools to act and
complete tasks. Then, when an agent is given a goal to complete, it loads up its
instructions (prompt). It gener ates a plan (internally) that includes which tools to execute
and how to execute them, along with which tools may require chaining tool output. Then it
observ es the results from the task and tool to determine if the goal is complete or if the
process needs to continue. If the goal is complete the agent returns the results. This is a
high-lev el overview of the process; some or all these steps may be more complicated in
practice.5
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 12 ---
For earlier foundationa l LLMs, (like GPT4), we had to emplo y some form of prompt
engineering tricks to get the model to reason and think step by step, to create a plan and
to execute tasks in that plan. Today, most models we use to build agents incorpor ate some
capacit y for reasoning and planning. This alleviates the need to inject reasoning with the
instructions provided to the agent; howev er, as we will see in Chapter 4, you may still want
to emplo y reasoning patterns to impro ve and control agent reasoning.
1.1.3 Agents act with tools
OpenAI and ChatGPT established a pattern for utilizing the LLM tool early on, and this
pattern has been adapted for agents. An LLM or agent must be instructed on how to use
tools, includ ing how to define the inputs and accept the outputs of a tool. Tools themselv es
are just extensions of functions, and we often refer to them as a tool function. Figure 1.4
illustr ates how an agent can be configured to utilize and subsequently invoke a tool
function.
Figure 1.4 For an agent to use a tool, that tool must first be registered with the agent in the form of a JSON
description/definition.l Once the tool is registered, the agent uses that tool in a process not unlike calling a
function in Python.
Tools often wrap around API calls, access databases, external applications, or other
resources. They allow the agent to act outside  its code and interact with external systems.
Various agent frameworks will enable you to register a tool using a decor ator function on
top of code that performs some tasks.6
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 13 ---
Classically , agents and LLMs were restricted in interacting with the tools they had within
their code base. Early on, this became proble matic because agent developers often spent
more time building tools than developing agents. Howev er, that has since changed, and
now protoco ls such as the Model Context Protocol (MCP) allow agents to interact with tools
outside of their code base.
1.2 Introducing the Model Context Protocol (MCP)
MCP, developed at Anthropic and released in November 2024, is an open standard created
by Anthropic, based on JSON-RPC 2.0, designed to let AI systems connect to external
services consistently , securely , and efficiently . Initially , it was promoted as an integr ation
point between LLMs as tools and an extension  to what OpenAI had already established with
ChatGPT as mentioned in the previous section. What separ ated it from earlier work and
established it as the pattern for LLMs was a simple, easy-to-adapt protocol that could wrap
any code base within a compact server. MCP fundamentally changes how we build AI
systems.
LLM and agent developers could thus use prebuilt MCP servers to integr ate tools outside
their code bases easily . They no longer had to concern themselv es with building individual
functions to act as tools and the mess of understanding interfaces, databases, and other
connections to resources. Instead, they could connect to an MCP server, discover all
available tools, and use them seamlessly . Figure 1.5 illustr ates how this works in practice,
eliminating the need for dev elopers to write custom tools.7
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 14 ---
Figure 1.5 An agent connects to an MCP server to discover the tools it hosts and the description of how to use
each tool. When an MCP server is registered with an agent it internally calls list_tools to find all the tools the
server supports and their descriptions. Then, like typical tool use internally, it can determine the best way to
use those tools based on the respective tool description.
Connecting agents to MCP servers typically involves setting up and running the server and
then registering with the agent. From there, when the agent is tasked with something, it
first calls list_tools  on the server to get a list of available tools and descriptions.
Internally , the agent then decides which tool to use, executes it, learns, revises, and
collects the results.
Often referred to as the USB-C for LLMs and agents, MCP is designed to address the
numerous problems frequently encountered when building LLM and agent applications:
Inconsistent tool access : While OpenAI creates a standard for tool use,
not all LLM pro viders adhere to this standard. Often, dev elopers will need
to adapt the JSON tool description based on the underlying model. MCP
abstracts all of that a way.    
Unreliable data responses : Using a standard protocol eliminates the
errors that can creep in when trying to implement and access multiple
tools. With MCP , response formatting is standardiz ed for easy
consumption and minimiz es problematic data errors.     
Fragmented integrations : Hosting tool function code alongside
agents/LLMs often meant writing code alongside the agent or creating
separ ate modules or packages that could later be consumed. Often, this
meant there w as a mix of code that supported v arious tools that would all
have to be connected when running an agent.     8
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 15 ---
Code extensibility : MCP pro vides a standard protocol for tool
development in an y language. This means dev elopers are no longer
constr ained and limited to Python or whatev er code base their agents
use.    
Implementation abstraction : Dev elopers no longer need to focus on
the complexit y of implementing tools. With the explosion of MCP , now
thousands of open-source serv ers are a vailable to pro vide tools for
virtually an y widespread use case.     
Easy to build : MCP and others ha ve provided numerous tools and
packages for quickly building serv ers in different languages. Now , if
developers need to dev elop their own tools, they simply need to wr ap
them in MCP , making those tools accessible to most agent and LLM
frameworks.     
MCP has been a major driver in the adoption of AI agents, as it enables the seamless
integr ation of a wide range of tools and resources. In chapter 3, we will examine MCP
extensiv ely, from building servers to consuming them with agents. Tools and MCP servers
are just one aspect of building agents; in the next section, we examine the fundamental
layers that build up agents.
1.3 Understanding the functional layers of an agent
Agents can be both elementary entities emplo yed to complete simple tasks and goals, to
more complex entities capable of underta king challenging, multi-step goals. Adding
functionalit y to an agent and enhancing its capabilities can be thought of as adding
functional la yers. Figure 1.6 shows the fiv e primary functional la yers of an agent.
Figure 1.6 the five functional layers of agents  Persona, Actions & Tools, Reasoning & Planning, Knowledge
& Memory, and Evaluation & Feedback9
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 16 ---
Each of these layers represents a progression in an agent's capabilities. Not all agents may
require, benefit from, or need all layers, and in some cases, specific layers may be omitted.
In most cases, though, the core layers (Persona, Tools & Actions, and Reasoning &
Planning) are essential to all agents.
We will examine each of these layers in more detail in the following sections. As we
progress through the book, each layer will be addressed by a paragraph or an entire
chapter . In the next section, we start with the core la yer of agents, the P ersona.
1.3.1 The Agent Persona
The agent Persona represents the base description and role of the agent. The persona,
often referred to as the system prompt, guide s an agent in completing tasks, learning how
to respond, and unders tanding other nuances. It includes elements such as background or
role (e.g., coder, writer). It may consist of demogr aphics and can be gener ated through
methods such as handcr afting, LLM (Large Language Model) assistance, or data-driv en
techniques, including evolutionary algorithms. Figure 1.7 shows the Persona layer as the
first la yer of an agent.
Figure 1.7 The Persona layer of an agent is the core layer, consisting of the system instructions that define
the role of the agent, and how it should complete goals and tasks. It may include how to reason/plan and
access knowledge and memory
We will explore how to create effectiv e and specific agent personas through techniques such
as prompt engineering,  reasoning and planning, and rubrics and grounding, as well as learn
the differences between human-formulated and AI -formulated (LLM) personas.10
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 17 ---
1.3.2 Agent Actions & Tools
The next essential layer for almost all agents is Actions and Tools. Tools empower agents to
not only complete tasks to undertak e an end goal but may also facilitate activities in the
higher layers of Reasoning & Planning, Knowl edge & Memory , and Evaluation & Feedback.
Figure 1.8 illustr ates the T ools & Actions la yer and its role in empowering an agent.
Figure 1.8 The role of Actions & Tools within the agent, and how tools can also help power the other agent
layers. Tools are a core extension of agents but are also fundamental to the functions used in the upper agent
layers
Tools & Actions can be categoriz ed into task completion, reasoning/planning,
knowledge/memory , and feedback/ev aluations, with varying levels of impact on the agent's
environment and internal states. Tool use within an agent can be driven through planning,
memory recollection, and evaluation/feedback mechanisms that influence the agent's
behavior and enhance learning.
1.3.3 Agent Reasoning & Planning
Agents that use tools or execute actions need to be able to reason and plan. Current
foundational models can effectiv ely reason on their own and provide their internal
monologue of thoughts. Except for most gener al applications, we want to have more control
over how an agent thinks. Figure 1.9 illustr ates the Reasoning & Planning layer and its role
in controlling the thought and planning process of agents.11
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 18 ---
Figure 1.9 The Reasoning & Planning of agents and how agentic thinking may be augmented. Reasoning may
come from many forms, from the underlying model powering the agent, to prompt engineering and even
through the use of tools
Within planning, agent s may emplo y either single-path reasoning, sequentially reasoning
through each step of a task, or multipath reasoning, exploring multiple strategies, saving
efficient ones for future use, such as a tree of thought. External planners' actions, which
can be code or other agent systems, ma y also pla y a role in orchestr ating plans.
In addition to developing essential skills for effectiv e agent task execution and goal
achiev ement, we will gain a deep understanding of the fundamental planning approaches. .
We will also explore diverse reasoning strategies, from single-path reasoning to multipath
reasoning, enabling us to optimiz e planning processes and consider multiple paths for
efficient task completion.
1.3.4 Agent Knowledge & Memory
Agents utilize knowledge and memory to annotate context with the most pertinent
information while limiting the number of tokens used. Knowledge and memory structures
can be unified, where both subsets follow a single structure or a hybrid structure involving
a mix of different retrie val forms. Knowledge and memory formats can vary widely , ranging
from sources (such as PDF documents) to databases (relational, object, or document),
graph databases, keyword search, dense embeddings, and simplif ying semantic similarit y
search through v ector representations, or ev en simple lists serving as agent memories.12
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 19 ---
Knowledge and memory work through a previously well-established pattern called
retriev al augmented gener ation (RAG) where  an agent retriev es information from various
knowledge and memory sources to augment its context and understanding. Numerous
forms of memory and knowledge can be implemented using multiple sources. Figure 1.10
highlights the importance of the Knowledge & Memory la yer within agents.
Figure 1.10 The Knowledge & Memory layer and how it interacts with and uses the same common forms of
storage across both types. Agent knowledge represents information the LLM was not initially trained with but
is later augmented. Likewise, memories represent past experiences and interactions of the user, agent or
even other systems.
We will acquire valuab le skills for optimizing information management within agents. We
will explore  the versatilit y of semantic embedding structure, allowing us to adapt to the
specific needs of agents, whether unified or hybrid. Additionally , we will gain expertise in
leveraging diverse data sources for knowledge , ranging from language-based documents to
various database types and Semantic similarit y-matching of word/document embeddings.
Finally , we will examine the significance of enabling hybrid structures to enhance both
memory and knowledge retriev al in agents.13
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 20 ---
1.3.5 Agent Evaluation & Feedback
The Evaluation & Feedback layer represents  external mechanisms that can help agents
provide more robust and accur ate responses. Internally , agents utilize a form of evaluation
and feedbac k that occurs during the Learn phase of the agent process loop, where an agent
evaluates the results of an action/tool and gener ates internal feedback to continue with or
modif y a goal execution plan. Figure 1.11 illustr ates the details of how Evaluation &
Feedback can be implemented as a final hardening la yer for agents.
Figure 1.11 The Evaluation & Feedback layer and the mechanisms used to provide them. From tools which
may help evaluate tool use, knowledge retrieval (grounding) and provide feedback, to other agents and
workflows that provide similar functionality
The mechanisms we emplo y for evaluation and feedback within an agent system vary
depending on the use case and desired solutio ns. These mechanisms may take the form of
tools that an agent can call upon other agents, either as a tool, a handoff , or through
guardr ails. There are even explicit agent workflow forms that encour age and promote
evaluation and feedback, as we will see in the next section.
1.4 Advancing onto multi-agent systems
Agents alone are powerful entities that can perform numerous tasks to achiev e multiple
goals. Howev er, at some point, single agents cannot handle the complexit y of solving more
advanced goals and multiple diverse tasks. Multi- agent systems can extend agents' power
and capabilit y to automate and solve complex problems. In the next section we will begin
to explore the first most common pattern for connecting agents.14
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 21 ---
1.4.1 The agent-flow assembly line
There are several ways in which multiple agents can be configured to work together . The
pattern you choose to define how you collabo rate with various agents will depend on your
use case. Figure 1.12 shows the agent -flow (assembly line) pattern for building a multi-
agent system.
Figure 1.12 The agent flow pattern of assembly with multiple agents. The flow starts with a planning agent
that breaks down the goal into a high-level plan that it then passed to the research agent, which may execute
the research tasks on the plan and after completing will pass to the content agent, which is responsible for
completing the later tasks of the plan, such as writing a paper based on the research
In the agent -flow pattern, agents are assembled like an assembly line. Each agent
represents a specializ ed worker with unique access to tools and resources. Agents may
share a typical frame of reference, such as a shared conversation thread or a blackboard,
or they ma y only pass on their output to the next agent, much lik e an assembly line.
The agent-flow pattern is the simplest to implement and control. It is often used to
automate well-defined, multi-step tasks with designated roles. Single- agent systems that
struggle to complete a chain of work are ideal candidates to upgrade to the agent -flow
pattern.
1.4.2 Agent orchestrations (hub-and-spoke)
Agent orche strations, often called hub-and-spok e, are a good upgrade when a single agent
becomes overloaded and needs to handle multiple goals. These agents are usually
interactive and may resemble assistants. Figure 1.13 shows the typical agent orchestr ation
pattern implemented to handle numerous goals.15
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 22 ---
Figure 1.13 The agent orchestration pattern, often referred to as hub-and-spoke. In this pattern, a central
agent asks as the hub or orchestrator to delegate tasks to each of work agents. Worker agents complete their
respective tasks and return them to the hub, which determines when the goal is complete and outputs the
results.
In the orchestr ation agent pattern, the primary agent is often responsible for planning and
coordinating with the other agents. The orchestr ator delegates tasks to the specializ ed
worker agents, not unlike using tools. Each specializ ed agent may perform multiple tasks to
complete sub-goals, and internally , they ma y also perform planning as required.
The benefit of using this pattern is that you can control all input and output through a
single agent. It is also easy to transform a single agent that uses tools into an orchestr ator
that now uses worker agents as tools. In turn, each worker agent now specializ es in a set
of tools.
A drawback  to using this pattern is that the worker agents are often tightly restricted
and cannot provide feedback, evaluation, or other forms of collabor ation. Variations to this
pattern emplo y agents as observ ers and critics, but these are still constr ained to
communication through delegation. As we will see in the next section, agent collabor ation is
a powerful counterpart that can o vercome these deficiencies.
1.4.3 Agent collaboration (teams of agents)
Early forms  of building multi- agent systems emplo yed the concept of a team of agents.
Each agent member took on the role in a team responsible for solving a complex problem.
Figure 1.14 shows an example of a multi- agent collabor ative team of agents.16
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 23 ---
Figure 1.14 A team of collaborative agents. The agent collaboration pattern allows agents to interact as peers
to allow back and forth communication from one agent to another. In some cases, a manager agent may work
as a user proxy and help keep collaborating agents on track
In the collabor ation pattern, all agents typically communicate with each other or a set of
specific counterpart agents. Like the previou s patterns, a specific agent role will have
access to a particular set of tools to work within its role. Unlike previous patterns, agents
have a role that uses a set of tools but ma y also ha ve some function within the team.
Within the figures' example team, the QA agent may validate that the code works
according to the produ ct's requirements. Still, it may also go beyond this and provide
criticism about the structure and other factors  of the code. Likewise, the product agent may
have the role of buildi ng requirements but also validating that requirements have been
completed to specification, according to the feedback of the QA.
The benefit of building collabor ation agent systems is that they can solve very complex
tasks over time and are relativ ely easy to envision. Howev er, the disadv antage is that such
systems are chatty and repetitiv e, meaning they are much less efficient in achieving goals
and ma y be o verly costly .
We typically only use this pattern when you are looking to solve complex problems that
would not otherwise be solvable. Indeed, agent collabor ation has been shown to have the
ability to gener ate new thoughts and ideas. Advanced patterns of collabor ation agents have
even used evolutionar y algorithms to exten d the ability to create new concepts even
farther .17
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 24 ---
It is also possible to mix and match these  patterns to create complex multi- agent
systems. This book will primarily focus on the OpenAI Agents SDK, which handles the
agent -flow and agent orchestr ation patterns well. In a later chapter , we will explore two
other frameworks, AutoGen and CrewAI, for building collabor ation agent systems. For the
next chapter we begin to look at building single agents using the OpenAI Agents SDK.
1.5 Summary
An AI agent has agency , the abilit y to mak e decisions, undertak e tasks,
and act autonomously on behalf of someone or something, powered b y
large language models connected to tools, memory , and planning
capabilities.     
An agents agency pro vides them the abilit y to process with an
autonomous loop called Sense-Plan- Act-Learn process.     
Assistants use tools to perform single tasks with user appro val, while
agents ha ve the agency to reason, plan, and ex ecute multiple tasks
independently to achiev e higher -level goals.     
The four patterns we see LLMs being used in include: direct user
interaction with LLMs, assistant pro xy (reformulating requests), assistant
(tool use with appro val), and autonomous agent (independent planning
and ex ecution).     
Agents receiv e goals, load instructions, reason out plans, identif y
required tools, ex ecute steps in sequence, and return results, all while
making autonomous decisions.     
Agents use actions, tool functions (extensions that wr ap API calls,
databases, and external resources) to act bey ond their code base and
interact with external systems.     
Model Context Protocol (MCP), dev eloped b y Anthropic in No vember
2024, serv es as the "USB -C for LLMs," pro viding a standardiz ed protocol
that allows agents to connect to MCP serv ers, disco ver available tools,
and use them seamlessly without custom integr ation code.     
MCP addresses inconsistent tool access, unreliable data responses,
fragmented integr ations, code extensibilit y limitations, implementation
complexit y, and pro vides easy -to-build standardiz ed serv ers.    
AI Agent dev elopment can be expressed in terms of fiv e functional la yers:
Persona, T ools & Actions, R easoning & Planning, Knowledge & Memory ,
and Ev aluation & F eedback.     
The P ersona la yer represents the core role/personalit y and instructions an
agent will use to undertak e goal and task completion.     
The T ools & Actions la yer pro vides the agent with the functionalit y to
interact and manipulate the external world.     
The R easoning & Planning la yer enhances an agent's abilit y to reason and
plan through complex goals that ma y require trial- and-error iter ation.     18
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 25 ---
The Knowledge & F eedback la yer represents external sources of
information that can augment the agent s context with external
knowledge or relate past experiences (memories) of previous
interactions.     
The Ev aluation & F eedback la yer represent external agent mechanisms
that can assist in impro ved response accur acy, encour age goal/task
learning and increased confidence in o verall agent output.     
Multi- agent systems include patterns such as Agent -flow assembly lines
(sequential specializ ed work ers), agent orchestr ation hub- and-spok e
(centr al coordinator with specializ ed work ers), and agent collabor ation
teams (agents communicating and working together with defined roles).     
The Agent -Flow pattern (sequential assembly line) is the most
straightforw ard multi- agent implementation where specializ ed agents
work sequentially lik e an assembly line, ideal for well-defined multi-step
tasks with designated roles.     
The Agent Orchestr ation pattern is a hub- and-spok e model where a
primary agent plans and coordinates with specializ ed work er agents,
transforming single- agent tool use into multi- agent delegation.     
The Agent Collabor ation pattern represents agents in a team-based
approach. Agents communicate with each other , provide feedback and
criticism, and can solv e complex problems through collectiv e intelligence,
though with higher computational costs and latency .    
AI agents represent a fundamental shift from tr aditional progr amming to
natur al language-based interfaces, enabling complex workflow
automation from prompt engineering to production-ready agent
architecture.     19

Now we roll up our sleeves and start building. In the last chapter , we provided a high-lev el

2.1 Understanding Large Language Models
Large Language Models (LLMs) have become ubiquitous in AI. AI Agents powered with LLMs
increasingly demonstr ate the power of this combination, which we will use throughout this
book.20
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 27 ---
While LLMs are often perceiv ed as black boxes, and we may not entirely understand how
they work, we know enough to unleash their power . In the next section, we will examine
how LLMs will power our future agents and agentic systems.
2.1.1 LLMs: Probabilistic Token Machines
At the found ation, LLMs consume text and then output text based on the learned probabilit y
of the next piece of text. Since machine  learning models dont understand text,
tokenization converts text to tokens, which are then converted to numbers based on
index es in the model's vocabulary . The model then sees this block of tokens (index
numbers) and outputs predictions for the next token, which is then used to sample a token,
which is Output.
Figure 2.1 demonstr ates the tr aining and inference/gener ation process of an LLM.21
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 28 ---
Figure 2.1 The training cycle and inference/generation process of a large language model. On the left is the
training process of the model where documents are ingested to train the LLM in a first pass. On the right, a
user enters a prompt which is first tokenized and fed into the model which then outputs probabilities it uses
to sample and produce the next token.
As the figure shows, training an LLM consumes blocks of text from various text sources and
is trained to predict the next best or expected token (token index). It does this by taking
the model's prediction for the next token and calculating the loss or amount of error. Error,
is a measure of how much the output token varies from the expected output token. This
error is fed back into the model to correct itself and predict the expected token. In practice,
the training process goes through billions of iterations and different levels of refinement
using methods lik e reinforcement learning with human feedback.22
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 29 ---
This extensiv e training outputs a language model that can consume tokenized text and
gener ate probabilities of the next token. Where the model uses gener ated probabilities to
sample and output the next token, a model will continue gener ating probabilities until it
encounters an EOS (end-of -stream) tok en, which halts the process.
The inference/gener ation process is fast in function, and tokens/text will be Output
quickly . This gives the illusion of intelligence, but in fact, the model is just predicting the
next best token. The actual intelligence of the model is the internal probabilit y map built
through man y training iter ations.
NO TE: REINF OR CEMENT LEARNING WITH HUMAN FEEDBA CK (RLHF) Toda y, large language models
will un der tak e more soph isticat ed training that fine tunes the model using tec hniques lik e
Reinf orcement Learning with Human Feedbac k (RLHF) to be tter output tok ens based on impr oved
probabilities, thusimpr oving a models reasoning capability . If you are int erest ed in learning more
about fine tuning models and RLHF , consult other great titles lik e Build a Large Language Model
from Scratc h.
The key takeaway here is to understand that an LLM works in terms of probabilities of
predicting tokens., so we must consider tokens and token prediction when constructing
prompts and instructing agents. But first we need to understand what a tok en is.
2.1.2 What is a token?
We use the term tokenization to discuss the process of breaking up text into bite-siz ed
pieces that an LLM can consume. These tokens-- words and word parts-- are then converted
to numerica l indices in the LLM's vocabulary . Then the index numbers are fed into the LLM,
which outpu ts predictio ns of token index es, sampled and Output as the next predicted
token.
Figure 2.2 demonstr ates the tokenization process for a block of JSON text compared with
regular text.23
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 30 ---
Figure 2.2 A comparison of tokenization of regular text compared to JSON.
The figure shows that the JSON is tokenized into 13 tokens, while the equiv alent text is
tokenized into six tokens. This indicates that text length is not a good indicator of the
number of tok ens.
This doesn t mean we should never use JSON in a prompt or for exchanging data with an
LLM. It shows the expense of using JSON in both the number of tokens and the overhead it
can gener ate. Most LLMs have techniques for handling JSON well now, but that wasnt
always the case.
The key takeaway is that raw text length does not indicate token length. LLM providers
(OpenAI, Anthropic) typically charge more to output tokens than the input by a factor of 2
to 1. This cost can be a concern in large agentic systems where thousands to millions of
tokens can be input and Output of an LLM.
Python packages like Tiktoken can help you measure the length of tokens in text. You
pass the text input to Tiktok en, which will output the number of tokens based on the
model's vocabulary encoding format. Howev er, the OpenAI Agents SDK we will use later
also pro vides an automatic measure of input and output tok ens for each LLM inter action.
The number  of token inputs will also typically increase the uncertaint y of the LLM output.
Howev er, we can control this with prompting and, as we will see in the next section, other
ways to tune the LLM's Output.
2.1.3 Tuning Temperature, Top P, and more
Tokens are not the only things that can contribute to the model's Output. We can pass
several parameters into the model to help modif y the Output. Figure 2.3 shows these
parameters and a quick summary of how they affect the output response of an LLM.24
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 31 ---
Figure 2.3 The various parameters that can be used to modify an LLMs output. The right side of the figure
provides an expanded view of the models predicting and sampling the tokens to generate output. On the left
we see the various parameters that can be used to alter the sampling of the next token within a model.
The right side of the figure shows a close up of the LLM prediction and sampling blocks
from Figure  2.2. On the left we see how the parameters affect which areas of the LLM. The
sampling parameters (temper ature, top-p , presence penalt y, frequency penalt y) all affect
the samplin g block. Mean while, the max tokens parameter fixes the length of tokens output
from the model for a single response.

We often modif y and adjust these parameters based on the agent's role. For example,
agents that require strict and consistent Output, like coders, will set the temper ature to 0
and have a higher frequency penalt y to keep that Output consistent and concise.
Conversely , a creativ e agent, a writer , may set the temper ature to a value of 2 and put a
low presence penalt y to allow it to be inno vative but not repeat itself .
It is also essential to understand that these parameters only influence the model and are
not a strict limit. Just because you set the temper ature of a model to zero doesn t mean the
LLM will always outpu t the same answer . Furthermore, one parameter may override
another , such as setting the temper ature in validates an y top-p v alue.
In most cases, the only parameters you may modif y are temper ature, again based on
the agent's role and max tokens. Setting max tokens can limit the length of responses and
avoid expensiv e interactions. Max tokens may also function as a guardr ail to prevent longer
unexpected outputs. We often leave the other parameters at the default and instead rely on
controlling the model through prompt engineering, as seen in the next section.26

2.2 Controlling LLMs with prompt engineering (Agent Persona)
Unfortunately , many view prompt engineering as unnecessary or hype attached to LLMs,
while the opposite is true. Prompt engineering can guide your agents to more predictable,
responsible, and productiv e Output and help minimiz e costs, promote safety, and gener ally
prevent your agents from unleashing chaos. Undoubtedly , it is an essential element to
building successful agents and agentic system s, as we reviewed in Chapter 1, the Persona
or prompt represent the core functional layer of building agents and prompt engineering is
a major aspect of that.
2.2.1 Applying core prompt techniques
How you prompt an LLM, the language, key terms, and tone may vary from LLM to LLM, but
several key techniques have remained universal. These techniques work well across LLMs
because they take advantage of foundational training patterns all models share to some
extent. Following these patterns, we also build universal prompts and agents that can move
from LLM to LLM without modification.
Table 2.2 summariz es the techniques, with an example, and a description of how they
may apply to an agent s persona or base set of instructions that will drive the agent. This is
a table of gener al techniques for prompt engineering. We may find that some methods,
such as #4 Define the exact output format, dont apply to us in this book since we use the
OpenAI Agents SDK, which handles that automatically .27

2.2.2 Thinking like an LLM
An analogy often used to describe writing effectiv e prompts is to think about writing a
prompt as if you were going to give the instructions to a new____. Where ____ could be a
new emplo yee, researcher , writer , or anyone lacking any background experience
undertaking a task.That doesn t mean you have to write a manual. Still, you want to avoid
assumptions and be specific when describing the task. For example; write a story about
time travel that takes the traveler back to 1921 and is no more than 5 paragraphs in length
and uses a humorous, engaging tone in the first person.
This also means you can describe the task as you want the agent to perform it;example,
when perfor ming task X always remember to consider Y and Z. Gener ally, this is how the
LLM will execute it. Carry this a step further , and you can describe entire workflows in a
prompt, complete with decisions and an LLM, and more importantly , an agent will be able to
execute it.
Figure 2.4 shows an example workflow we want to convert into a prompt so that an LLM
or agent can ex ecute it as a person would.31
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 38 ---
Figure 2.4 A complex workflow showing what a search researcher may perform. The workflow illustrates the
tasks an agent may undertake, complete with decision points (circles) and the flow from one task to another.
(The figure is overly complex just to demonstrate the workflow.)In the figure, the workflow illustrates how we
want an LLM or agent to perform a series of tasks and how it considers the output to make decisions based
on the task results. The workflow details are not critical, and the complexity is used to demonstrate how
complex instruction prompts can be constructed by following good prompt engineering practices. (Dont be
alarmed if you cant follow this figure; it is just a toy example of complexity to the extreme.)
Tasks will often be tool calls that the LLM uses to perform functions or get new information.
While you may usually describe task loops in a prompt, in practical terms, an LLM will often
exit loops quickly , after three or fewer tries. Compare this to an agent, which will continue
looping until it is satisfied that the goal is complete or unattainable.32

Of course, creating prompts like this can be uncomfortable at first, so asking for assistance

2.3 Building an agent with OpenAI Agents
Numerous frameworks for building agents exist, each with a unique approach to
implementing an agent. Fortunately , protoc ols like MCP and A2A provide a common
foundation for how agents interact with tools and each other. We will focus on using OpenAI
Agents SDK in this book.
The folks at OpenAI took the opportunit y to wait and watch where other frameworks
succeeded or failed. As a result, the OpenAI SDK is robust, light, extensible, and flexible.
We will use this pattern to build all agents and dev elop our first agent in the next section.
2.3.1 Building a minimal agent
Listing 2.3 shows a starting example for a research planning agent and a first step to our
Deep Research agent workflow . This first agent researches the topic and outputs a concise
5-step plan. The code and instructions to run it and set up your Python environment can be
found at the book s GitHub repository: https://github . com/cxbxmx cx/ AI-Agent - Workflows36

#A import required packages
#B load environment variables from .env file

instructions = """    #C
You are a research planning assistant.
**TASK INSTRUCTIONS**
- You will be given a research topic.

- Output 5 concise tasks (5 words or less) to your plan.
"""

)

parameters when creating an agent.

2.3.3 Controlling inputs and typed outputs
Figure 2.5 shows a simple workflow defined for the agents and compares without

uses typed outputs, those outputs can feed into another agent without the risk of response
variabilit y breaking the workflow . Remember , LLMs are probabilistic token predicters, and
there is always a chance that outputs will vary. By strongly typing outputs and inputs, we

allows the JSON type to be strict. The problem was that the dict[int, str] object allowed
extra prope rties, breaking the JSON strict mode criteria. The issue was resolv ed by entering
the code into ChatGPT and allowing it to search for and recommend a solution. Avoid the
option to disable strict JSON (strict_json_schema=False ) because this opens up potential

2.4 Enhancing agents through tool integration
Agents work best when  they have agency , that is the ability to make and execute decisions.
If we apply this rule to our current agent, the research planner , we can see it isnt an
agent. The only thing we have developed thus far is a prompt step. Building an AI workflow
with prompt steps is viable and called prompt chaining.
We want to develop flexible and adaptable agents, and this requires agency . The easiest
way to give agents agency is to provide them with tools they can decide when and where to
use. In the next section, we will look at adding tools to our agents.
2.4.1 Providing agents with tools
Tools are actions an agent may perform, and they may represent code functions, but they
may also calls or handoffs to other agents. We may build internal tools with the agent or
consume external tools through protocols like MCP (model context protocol). Chapter 3 will
examine the man y tools an agent can consume through MCP .
Figure 2.8 shows patterns an agent may use to consume tools. For this chapter , we will
focus on internal use within the same code base. In Chapter 3, we will take a closer look at
MCP and how we can use it to consume extern al tools. MCP allows tools and tool servers to
be run locally or hosted remotely and supplied o ver HT TP.46
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 53 ---
Figure 2.8 The various patterns for an agent to consume tools. Tools may be internal code functions or
external connections to MCP servers, hosted locally or remotely and connected through using MCP protocols.
The figure demonstr ates how an agent can execute tools, what a tool call looks like and
where those tools may reside. Tools can be local (within the code base) or hosted through a
process, or external over HTTP. This flexibili ty allows us many options for building and
selecting the tools are agents will consume.
Listing 2.11 shows how we would extend our simple research planning agent with a
get_research_sources  tool. We could add the tool, but a better practice is to be explicit in
the prompt instructions for and when to use a tool. The updated prompt now instructs the
agent first to pull the available research sources and create a plan that incorpor ates those
sources into each specific task.47

Listing 2.11 07_agent_with_tool.py
instructions = """    #A
You are a research planning assistant.
**TASK INSTRUCTIONS**
- You will be given a research topic.
- Begin by using the tool get_research_sources() 
to get a list of available research sources. 
- Constrain your research plan 
only to use the available research sources.
- Your task is to provide a plan for researching this topic.
- Output 5 concise tasks and specify which of the 
available research sources will be used for each task.
"""
class Task(TypedDict):
step: int
"""Task Step."""
research_source: str    #B
"""The source to search."""
description: str
"""Task description."""
class ResearchPlanModel(BaseModel):
tasks: list[Task]
"""Numbered tasks for research."""
model_config = ConfigDict(extra='forbid')
@function_tool    #C
def get_research_sources() -> list[str]:
"""Provides a list of research sources."""
search_sources = [    #D
"Wikipedia",
"Google",
"YouTube",
]
return search_sources    
agent = Agent(
name="Research Planner", 
instructions=instructions,
output_type=ResearchPlanModel,48
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 55 ---
For this example, we are returning a static list of available research sources, but a more
practical scenario would be to query the available resources. Then, the agent could build a
plan and search only available sources. Using patterns like this creates extensibilit y into our
agents from the ground up. Later, we can develop the query mechanism inside the tool to
provide a dynamic list of sources.
We can easily expose any internal function using the @function_tool  decorator. This
decor ator creates all the internal plumbing and gener ates the JSON tool description the
agent will use to decide how the tool is executed. It is important to remember that after
registering a tool with an agent, it will be included in all calls to the LLM, regardless of
whether the tool is used or not.
While you could easily provide a single agent with dozens of tools, in practical terms, you
typically want to limit the number to 5-10. There are several good reasons why you would
limit the number of tools an agent can use, as shown below:
Tools are overhead : always consider that a JSON description of ev ery
tool y ou register with an agent will be o verhead on each LLM call. Simply
put, each tool call adds tok ens to the input, which can be expensiv e if
your agents mak e repetitiv e calls to perform tasks other than work.     
Tools add complexity : Each tool y ou register with an agent essentially
adds more instructions to the prompt describing how to use the tool.
More instructions and longer prompts increase the v ariabilit y and
potential for failures in the system.     
Tools can fail : each tool adds another potential point of failure and
possible area y ou ma y need to reco ver. Robust agentic workflows try to
minimiz e the points of failure or pro vide backups and retries if things fail.     
Tools are decisions : when an agent uses a tool, it mak es a decision,
which can be both powerful and problematic. While allowing an agent to
decide to search for something is oka y, it ma y not be wise to giv e it a tool
that can delete files, for example. Be cognizant of what decisions y ou
empower y our agents to mak e and be comfortable with the results if they
make the wrong decision. If y ou allow an agent to delete files, expect it
to delete all the files it has access to .    
Tools are potent additio ns to agents, but they provide many opportunities for misuse and
points of failure. Therefore, limiting an agent's tool use to just what the agent needs is
essential. Fortunately , OpenAI, through tracing, provides us with a helpful way of
identif ying issues and problems using tools.#A updated prompt instructions that include mention of using the tool
#B add the research source to the task step object
#C wrap the function we want to expose as a tool with decorator
#D for this example just return a static list of research sources
#E register the tool with the agenttools=[get_research_sources],    #E
)49

instructions = """
You are a research planning assistant.

- You will be given a research topic.
- Begin by using the tool get_research_sources() 
to get a list of available research sources. 
- Use the tool get_resource_url()    #A
to look up the url for the research source.
- Constrain your research plan 
only to use the available research sources.
- Your task is to provide a plan for researching this topic.
- Output 5 concise tasks and specify which of the 
available research sources will be used for each task.
"""
class ResearchSource(TypedDict):    #B
name: str
"""Name of the search source."""
url: str
"""URL of the search source."""
class Task(TypedDict):
step: int
"""Task Step."""
research_source: ResearchSource    #B
"""The source to search."""
description: str
"""Task description."""

tasks: list[Task]
"""Numbered tasks for research."""

def get_research_sources() -> list[str]:
"""Provides a list of research sources."""

"Wikipedia",51

Now, the agent is instructed to first get the list of research sources and then use the new
get_resource_url to look up the URL for each source. This means the agent must make
multiple tool calls, some of which depend on the initial tool call. While this example is
contriv ed, it demonstr ates a t ypical pattern in agent tool use called tool chaining.
Tool chaining occurs when an agent with multiple tools needs to chain the action of one
tool to another . In some cases, the prompt instructions may define what that tool chaining
looks like, but often, we allow the agent the agency to determine which tools need to chain
into which.#A The agent is instructed to use the get_resource_url function to a get a resources URL
#B We add a sub type to hold details about the research source
#C New function that returns a lookup for the research sources URL
#D Add the new function to the list of tools the agent can use"Google",
"YouTube",
]
return search_sources   
@function_tool
def get_resource_url(research_soruce: str) -> str:    #C
"""Provides a url for the research source."""
search_sources = {
"Wikipedia": "https://www.wikipedia.org",
"Google": "https://www.google.com",
"YouTube": "https://www.youtube.com",
}
return search_sources[research_soruce] 
agent = Agent(
name="Research Planner", 
instructions=instructions,
output_type=ResearchPlanModel,
tools=[get_research_sources, get_resource_url],    #D
)
input = "learn about AI agents"
with trace("Deep Research Workflow (Tools)"):
result = Runner.run_sync(
agent, 
input=input,
)
print(result.final_output)52

Context Protocol for AI agents
This chapter covers
Under standing MCP fundamentals f or agent de velopment 
Ge tting S tar ted with MCP Ser vers
Actioning MCP Ser vers with A gents 
Building MCP Ser vers f or A gents 
In the last chapter , we examined an agent's core components. This chapter discusses the
Model Conte xt Protocol (MCP), the connector that empowers agents. MCP is often described
as the USB-C for agents and LLMs because it provides a standard protocol for agent tools.
More importantly , for us and our journey to developing complex research agent workflows,
it opens up a landscape of tools our agents can use.
MCP adoptio n in the AI space has been so quick and widespread that almost anything we
want our agents to do is likely supported by an MCP server. Not only that, but we can also
build our agents as consumable MCP servers, allowing us to connect multiple complex
agentic workflows as tools to other agent workflows so we can build agent specific
workflows that can be reused lik e components.
In the following sections, we will examine the fundamentals of MCP architecture and
then explor e building and consuming servers with agents to understand how MCP
transforms agent capabilities.57
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 64 ---
3.1 Understanding MCP fundamentals for agent development
Before diving into agent -specific implementations, we need to understand what MCP is, why
it exists, and how it fundamentally changes how we build AI systems. MCP is an open
standard created by Anthropic, based on JSON-RPC 2.0, designed to let AI systems connect
to external services consistently , securely , and efficiently .
3.1.1 The standardization problem MCP solves
Before MCP, developers building AI agents faced several critical challenges, making agent
development complex and brittle. Figure 3.1 highlights developers' main challenges when
building agent and LLM applications.
Figure 3.1 The leading challenges AI Agent developers face when building agents include fragmented tool
integration, inconsistant data access, complicated multiple-agent orchestration, and security and control.58
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 65 ---
As the figure shows, developers faced multiple challenges when building agents for LLMs.
The first problem was fragmented tool integration , or handling multiple ways of defining
tools, depen ding on the LLM provider. These challenges affected developers on projects of
all scales, from small single- agent systems to more complex multi- agent workflows.Since
each AI model or agent framework defines and calls tools differently , a tool built for
OpenAI's function calling, for example, wouldn't work with Anthropic's Claude without
significant modification. This meant constant reimplementation of the same functionalit y
across different platforms.
A second problem was inconsistent data access . Connecting agents to external data
sources required custom solutions for each source. Your agent might need one interface for
file systems , another for databases, and another for web APIs. Each integr ation was a
bespok e solution that w as hard to maintain and reuse.
Complex agent orchestration and communicating with external agents was another
obstacle.When you wanted multiple agents  to work together , each needed custom
integr ation code. No standard way existed for one agent to expose its capabilities to
another , making complex multi- agent workflows difficult to implement.
Finally , there were security and control issues . Without standardiz ed protocols, it was
challenging to implement consistent securit y measures, access controls, and monitoring
across different tools and data sources.
MCP solves all these problems by providing a unified protocol that standardiz es how
agents interact with external systems. Thus, agent development becomes more like
assembling standardiz ed components r ather than building ev erything from scr atch.
3.1.2 MCP architecture: Clients, servers, and services
Remember back before USB-C became the standard connection, when every device,
including phones, camer as, tablets, and headphones, had its proprietary connector? With
the adoption of USB-C, we can now connect easily using one cable for all devices that
support it, and not have to worry about individual cables and adapters. Without MCP, your
agent faces a similar challenge, where each tool or service you want to connect to requires
its own custom integr ation. Figure 3.2 demon strates the problem of connecting your agent
to multiple services and resources.59
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 66 ---
Figure 3.2 A typical problem agent/LLM developers face when connecting to multiple services and resources
is the lack of standardized connections and the need to support multiple different connectors to whatever
services they need access to.
The figure shows an example of an agent that needs to connect to multiple services and
resources. Before MCP, the agent developer would have constructed these tools and had to
manage the complexit y of connecting to APIs, SDKs, protocols, and formatting results. If
you wanted to build a capable agent, most of your work would include creating the tools to
power that agent.
MCP changed all that by introducing a strong communication protocol and a way to
abstract the messy tool implementations out of the agent code base. Now, all an agent
developer has to do is connect to the MCP server that wraps the service they want to
connect to, and everything else is automatic. Figure 3.3 shows the change from heavy
internal tool implementations to MCP for common external resources.60
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 67 ---
Figure 3.3 Implementing MCP as a service layer abstracts access to the various services that agent may want
to connect to and use.
Continuing with the single agent example, when using MCP, we only need to write a few
lines of code to connect the server and then register it with the agent. Most modern AI
agent frameworks now support seamless, simple integr ations with MCP. There is even a
growing communit y of dev elopers implementing MCP serv ers for connecting an y AI agent or
LLM.
To simplify this further , we consider the MCP ecosystem to come in three components:
the client, server, and service/resource. Figure 3.4 shows these components along with
examples of clients and services that can be integr ated.61
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 68 ---
Figure 3.4 The basic components of MCP architecture are the client, server, and services. Here you can see
some common clients (agents, LLM applications, Claude desktop, and VS code) and potential services (file
operations, database queries, web APIs, and other agents) that the clients might connect to.
The figure shows these basic components of the MCP architecture:
MCP client . MCP is not just for agents but is also a vailable for man y AI-
powered applications and LLMs. The client is responsible for connecting to
the serv er and using disco very resources to find tools and other resources
it can use and consume.     62
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 69 ---
MCP server . The serv er handles client requests and pro vides access to
tools, resources, and services. It processes requests and sends back
responses using the standardiz ed JSON-RPC 2.0 protocol.     
Service  represents the application or resource, from file oper ations,
database queries, web searches, or other AI agents. Using MCP to
connect to agents works the same as using a tool. This approach can
work well, but if y ou require enhanced functionalit y, Google s A2A (agent -
to-agent) protocol ma y be more suitable, which we will co ver in Chapter
7.    
Communication betwee n MCP Clients and Servers uses JSON-RPC 2.0, which ensures that
requests and responses  are exchanged consistently and structured regardless of the service
being accessed.
3.1.3 Core components: Tools, resources, and prompts
Every MCP server provides functionalit y through three types of components, each serving a
specific purpose in agent workflows: tools, resources, and prompts. Figure 3.5 illustr ates
these three components and their consumption.63
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 70 ---
Figure 3.5 The main components of an MCP server include prompts, specialized system instructions;
resources (used for access to files), configuration, and databases; and Tools, which are extensions of an
internals agents ability to consume and use tools
The MCP server provides discoverable endpoin ts, such as list_tools , that allow an LLM or
agent to identif y eleme nts within that component. Below is a summary of each component
an MCP serv er ma y expose:
Tools  are the actionable capabilities that agents and LLMs can in voke to
perform external tasks. T ools are gener ally the primary component of an
MCP serv er that an agent or an LLM will consume and use to complete
tasks. T ools ma y also serv e as resources or prompts if a particular agent
client is unable to disco ver and utiliz e them other components such as
resources or prompts.     
Resources  represent external databases, files, configur ation or other
entities that LLM/agents ma y consume.     64
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 71 ---
Prompts  function as pre-defined templates that standardiz e how agents
interact with complex workflows or specializ ed tools and resources the
server ma y provide.    
In this book, we will gener ally focus on using Tools from the MCP servers. The OpenAI
Agents SDK currently doesn t support resources and prompts, which is acceptable since
Tools can take on the role of resources and prompts. Howev er, if you use another agent
framework that supports resources or prompts, consider using these other components for
their intended purpose. In the next section, we examine how MCP servers can be deplo yed
and accessed.
3.1.4 MCP deployment patterns for agents
MCP servers can be deplo yed in different configur ations depending on your agent
architecture needs. Figure 3.6 shows the three ways an MCP server may be connected to
agents or an LLM: local deplo yment, remote deplo yment, or a hybrid architecture. Our
focus in this book will be primarily on how agents use MCP; howev er, it is essential to
remember that MCP is not just for agents.65
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 72 ---
Figure 3.6 The various deployment patterns that may be used to connect MCP servers to agents. From running
locally and within a child process on the same machine, running remotely and accessible over HTTP to include
hybrid architectures that blend local and remote MCP servers to single agent.
From the figure we see the various ways MCP servers can be deplo yed and consumed by
agents. Servers may run locally as a child process communicating over standard
input/output pipes, or remotely , be it external to the agent process on the same machine or
externally hosted over HTTP. You can also use a combination of local and remote MCP
servers connected in a h ybrid fashion.
Below is a breakdown of the various deplo yment patterns you may use to communicate
to agents, but these patterns ma y also be used b y LLM applications:
Local Deployment (STDIO Transport)  When MCP servers run on the same machine
as your agent, they communicate through standard input/output streams. This approach
offers:
Low latency communication     
No network o verhead     
Simple process management     66
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 73 ---
Ideal for dev elopment and single-machine deplo yments     
Remote Deployment (SSE Transport)  When servers run on different machines,
communication happens o ver HT TP using Serv er-Sent Ev ents (S SE). This enables:
Distributed agent architectures     
Shared tool serv ers across multiple agents     
Scalable, cloud-nativ e deplo yments     
Multi-tenancy and load balancing     
Hybrid Deployments  Real-world agent systems often combine both patterns:
Local serv ers for sensitiv e oper ations (file access, local databases)     
Remote serv ers for shared services (web search, external APIs)     
Agent -to-agent communication through remote MCP serv ers    
While our primary focus will be on agents with MCP, it can be quite helpful to examine how
LLM applications, such as Claude desktop , can utiliz e MCP serv ers.
3.1.5 MCP powers the functional agent layers
While MCP will gener ally be consumed as a tool, we should not limit ourselv es to thinking of
it as just a tool. MCP can be integr ated into the functional layers we use to describe agents,
as we discussed in Chapter 1. Figure 3.7 demonstr ates how agents may use MCP through
the various la yers.
Figure 3.7 MCP can be used to add functionality in the form of tools to all the functional agent layers.67
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 74 ---
From the figure we can see how the functional agent layers may be supported or enhanced
by tools hosted on MCP servers. Each layer will consume a specific aspect or type of tool.
For Tools & Actions layer the focus is access  to applications and APIs. The Reasoning &
Planning layer access tools/serv ers that enhance reasoning/planning such as Sequential
Thinking. For Knowledge & Memory , tools will typically connect to various storage
containers such as files, databases, index es and possibly other agents. Finally , Evaluation &
Feedback may use feedback tools, evaluation agents or specific agent workflow patterns
that encour age direct feedback connected through MCP .
Remember , MCP represents a connection pattern for agents that is not limited to tools
and actions, but can also support functionalit y across all agent layers from
reasoning/planning to knowledge/memory and ev aluation/feedback.

def get_research_sources() -> list[str]:

search_sources = [
"Wikipedia",
"Google",
"YouTube",
]
return search_sources

3.2.3 Understanding MCP transport types
We have already explored the two communication transport types that MCP supports:
STDIO and SSE. The difference between these types is significant when consuming and
building MCP clients or servers. Table 3.1 provides a detailed breakdown of each transport
type, its underlying protocol, and its most suitable applications.
Table 3.1 Differences between STDIO and SSE transports for MCP servers
Name How it works When you should use it

subprocess  on the same host and
exchanges JSON-RPC 2.0 messages
over the process s stdin / stdout  pipes.

link is low -latency but strictly 1-to-1 :
only the parent process that spa wned
the serv er can talk to it. Local dev elopment or quick CLI
experiments where y ou want to k eep
everything in a single shell session.
 Command-line tools or scripts that spin
up a helper MCP serv er just for the task at
hand (e.g. , mcp dev my_server.py ).
 Containers that y ou run inter actively (
docker run -it  ) and communicate with
via stdio to a void opening ports.
 Simple process-to-process integr ations
that don t need remote access, rev erse
proxies, or multi-client support
SSEThe MCP serv er runs as an HTTP
service . Clients POST JSON-RPC
requests to /messages , while the serv er
streams responses and notifications
back o ver a long-liv ed text/event-
stream  connection (Serv er-Sent Ev ents)
such as /sse. This mak es the channel
half-duplex (client  server via HT TP
POST, serv er  client via S SE) but fully
network -addressable and capable of
serving man y clients concurrently Remote or cloud deplo yments where

proxies.

3.3 Actioning MCP servers for Agents
MCP servers embody the actions an agent can undertak e. Agent actions represent any acts
an agent can perform to complete a goal. An action may be using a tool, finding a resource,
or even handing off or engaging other agents.
We will use MCP to build and host our own tools, access APIs, interface with various data
sources, and serve agents' actions. Figure 3.13 illustr ates the different methods for
constructing and consuming MCP serv ers.77
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 84 ---
Figure 3.13 Shows the various ways an agent may interact with and consume MCP servers
As the figure shows, there are several ways in which we consume and use MCP to provide
agents with tools or other actions. MCP servers may be locally hosted and run within a sub-
process of the agent process that communicates using STDIO (standard pipes
input/output). Alternativ ely, MCP servers may be run external to the agent process and
connected by SSE over HTTP but still run on the same machine/container . Or, MCP servers
may run externally hosted outside the machine, perhaps as a cloud resource accessed with
SSE over HT TP.

As long as the block runs, the subprocess will remain active and the tool will be accessible.

3.3.2 Actioning local MCP servers over SSE with agents

because the FastMCP  class in Listing 3.1 can support STDIO and SSE. When running the file
in process, the server defaults to STDIO , but when run externally through a command, we
can set it to SSE. This allows us to keep the MCP server running and have multiple agents
connect.

By adding the MCP Filesystem server, we now give the agent access to the script's folder .

reason, y ou should gener ally be careful isolating the folder y ou pro vide to this serv er.
Gener ally, when using any MCP server, you want to validate and verify its functions and
operation. You also need to understand what a server can do on behalf of your agent.
Agents are often beneficial, but like a nave child, their interpretation of help may not
always lead to the best outcomes.
Fortunately , there are several ways to mitigate and avoid risks, such as building your
own MCP servers. Building your servers allows  you to consume resources that you may not
have access to, or as a way to control access to various MCP tools. As we will see in the
next section, building y our own MCP services giv es you control o ver your agents' actions.
