4  Architecting and building
multi-agent syst ems
This chapter covers
Archit ecting multi-agent syst ems 
Balancing agents with agentic f lows 
Under standing handof fs in agent f lows 
Validating agent f lows with guardrails 
Shortly after single AI agent systems emerged, developers and researchers quickly began
adding more agents. The concept was simple: more agents meant more difficult goals could
be tackled and solved. They experimented with numerous patterns, from agent armies and
armadas to collabor ative agent teams and various other architectures. Over time and
through pr actical, real- world use, sev eral key architectures emerged.
In this chapter , we explore the main practical patterns for building multi- agent systems.
We will look at why and when we may need to move from single- agent systems to more
complex workflows. Then we delve into how agents communicate and transition from one
another , and how we can monitor and protect agent -to-agent communication.
4.1 Architecting multi-agent systems
Multi- agent architectur es are more powerful than single- agent systems. If not emplo yed
effectiv ely, though, multiple agents are often more costly , have increased latency , and are
more unpredictable. With due diligence and following best practices, multi- agent
architectures become easier to dev elop and maintain.97
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 104 ---
Through research and practical use, three  multi- agent architecture patterns have
emerged for building these systems. We already looked at these key architectures in
chapter one, but we will review them again . Figure 4.1 demonstr ates the three main
architectures: the flow , orchestr ation (hub and spok e), and collabor ation.
Figure 4.1 The three well-established patterns for building multiple agent systems, from the agent flow
(assembly line), orchestrator (hub-and-spoke), to the collaboration (peer to peer).
Before getting into these patterns further , lets look at what decision-making, control, and
communication mean to agent systems.
Decision-making . For LLMs and agents, a decision maker makes decisions based on its
available knowledge of the system. When deciding which agents have decision-making
authorit y, we also want to make sure they have the best knowledge (context) to make
those decisions. Of course, having too much context can make it difficult for an LLM to
make effectiv e decisions, so we may also want to control what context is available to a
decision-making agent.
Control . The agent or LLM with control is the this is the one performing the work. They
are executing tasks with tools to either produce more context or knowledge, or to perform
actions. In agentic systems, control will often flow from agent to agent in sequential or
parallel flows.98
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 105 ---
Communication. Its importa nt to address the way agents communicate with each
other and pass on context. In some cases, such as collabor ation patterns, you may want
this commu nication to be open to all agents in a form of shared context. Alternativ ely, you
may find this shared knowledge to be a detriment to an agent's ability to make decisions.
In this case, you would choose more closed or concise forms of communication between
agents.
In the flow pattern, decision-making, control, and comm unication are executed in an
aassembly line, flowing from agent to agent. In the orchestrator  pattern, decision-making is
centralized and communication flows from the central agent, while control is passed from
the hub to the worker as needed. Finally , the collaboration  pattern allows decision -making
and control to pass from agent to agent, and all agents to communicate through a
centralized channel. T able 4.1 summariz es these details.
Table 4.1 Key concepts, definitions, and applications to agentic behavior
Concept How does it relate to agents How it applies to agents
Decision-making
(command)The agent can mak e all k ey decisions
on their own and is only constr ained
by the tools and resources a vailable
to them.Agents without decision-
making will rely on others to
reason and plan tasks they
may perform.
Control The agent is giv en the abilit y to
execute tools and perform actions.Without control, agents don t
perform actions or use tools
Communication Represents the abilit y for the agent
to communicate with other agentsCommunication between
agents ma y be completely
open or restricted to share
only what is necessary
These three core agentic patterns represent foundational strategies that can be altered,
mixed, and decompose d into more complex patterns. Your experience will often dictate the
choice of which pattern to use with the particular use case. For that reason, you will usually
want to start building flows, rather than progressing to orchestr ation/collabor ation and
other custom complex coordination strategies. Before building any patterns, though, it is
essential to understand  how decision-making, control, and communication will flow through
your agent architecture.
4.1.1 Decision-making for agent systems
Decision-making (command) and control are central to any agentic system, so lets dig into
some more complex examples that demonstr ate different strategies for these two
elements. Figure 4.2 shows three common agent decision (command) and control patterns
mapped to an agent flow and orchestr ator/manager multi- agent architectures.99
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 106 ---
Figure 4.2 Decision-making (command) and control demonstrated for more specialized multi-agent
architectures, the flow, orchestration and manager architectures.100
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 107 ---
At the top of the figure is the agent flow, where no central decision-making and control
occur. Next, in the orchestr ation pattern, the decision maker is the orchestr ator, who
delegates tasks (control) appropriate to the worker agents. Finally , the manager -worker
pattern is a more gener al extension of the orchestr ator pattern, where hierarchical
relationships between manager agents transfer command down to sub-managers and
worker agents, who alw ays keep control to ex ecute tasks.
As a gener al rule, more complicated patterns will provide more control, provided you
understand how to manage the three elements of DM/Control/Communication. If you
struggle with delegation patterns (orchestr ator, manager -worker), for example, stick to
using the flow pattern. Agent patterns are a construct for building multi- agent systems;
you can apply any pattern to any use case, but as we will see below , some patterns are
more effectiv e than others.
4.1.2 Communicating with shared-memory, message-passing, and MCP
Communication is a core element in regulating how much or little information you may give
your agents access to. The reason we limit information access to agents is to prevent
unnecessary context that is costly or may confuse agents. To understand this, consider a
worker agent who is tasked with just completin g two or three tool executions. Providing this
agent with all previous communication would likely force the agent to consider alternate
plans, resulting in different tasks being completed.
The typical  communication strategies we see in agent systems include passing
messages/objects as needed, but may also consist of all agents observing a central
conversational thread. Likewise, agents may communicate with each other through the
growing development of protocols such as MCP. Figure 4.3 shows these methods of agent
communication put into pr actice.101
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 108 ---
Figure 4.3 Comparison of different agent communication patterns represented in a flow.
At the top of the figure is a typical flow communication pattern where all messages and
objects are passed from one agent to the next. In some cases, we may not want all
information and instead limit middle worker agents to just the necessary messages to
complete tasks. Conversely , the shared memory or conversation channel communication
pattern provides the same access to all agents. To the bottom of the figure, where
communication is restricted to an agent being  used as a tool over MCP or provided through
a function.102
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 109 ---
As with decision making and control, you can combine, mix, and match agent
communication patterns. Sometimes this may be intentional because you want the benefit
of multiple patterns over an entire complex workflow . Gener ally, you will want to align
yourself with one pattern and use it across agents in the same code base.
4.1.3 Channeling multi-agent coordination strategies
Command, control, and communication are the first three Cs of building agents. Howev er,
there is a fourth C, coordination, that defines how agents may coordinate execution from a
single path to parallel or multiple paths of execution. Figure 4.4 shows the four examples of
how we ma y coordinate agents to ex ecute inside the flow and orchestr ation patterns.103
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 110 ---
Figure 4.4 Various ways agents may coordinate execution sequentially or in parallel.104
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 111 ---
The figure shows the four most common strategies for assembling agents to execute tasks
and coordinate output. At the top, sequential flow or agent-to-agent pipeline  executes
agents one after the other. Next, we have a modified parallel flow where the two middle
agents are executed in parallel. Beneath that we see a modified orchestr ation pattern called
hierarchical coordinatio n, where some worker agents are executed in parallel and others
sequentially . Finally , at the bottom, the iterative debate and refinement  approach is another
variation of a recursiv e flow pattern where a critic agent acts as a gate to control execution
until the goal is complete.
We can combine agents  different ways, depend ing on the situation. You can also chain or
embed these strategie s within other strategies, which can lead to very complex agent
workflows. But you will always want to start with the most straightforw ard approach and
pattern that work, and then refine and iter ate later .
Remember , agents work best when they can have agency and make decisions, and we
should avoid structuring agents to hinder that agency . Its usually best to rely on agents to
do the work and complete tasks as they deem appropriate. So we should first evolve any
single agent to use the flow pattern, which then can be customiz ed and adapted in many
ways.
Lets take a closer look at the execution and coordination strategies shown in figure 4.4,
along with a few more y ou ma y find useful.
SEQUENTIAL PIPELINE, AGENT TO AGENT
In a sequential flow, agents are arranged in a linear pipeline, where each agent performs
one stage of the task and passes its output to the next agent. This is a simple,
deterministic flow. For example, one agent might gener ate data, the next analyz es it, and a
final agent summariz es the results.
This approa ch is well-suited for tasks natur ally broken into ordered stages or a fixed
workflow . It is commonly used in data proces sing or document workflows (e.g. cleaning 
analysis   summarizat ion) where each steps output feeds the next. It provides a clear,
easy-to-tr ace structure.
A sequential flow is inefficient if subtasks could be done in parallel or if a single agent
could handl e multiple steps. Since each stage  waits for the previous step to be completed,
this pattern  can be slow and brittle: if one stage fails, the whole pipeline stalls. Not ideal for
tasks requiring iter ation or backtr acking, since the flow is one- way.
PARALLEL DELEGATION
Multiple agents can also work on sub-tasks or portions of a task simultaneously before
merging results. Independent tasks are delegated to different agents to execute in parallel.
A downstream agent or step then combines or synthesiz es their outputs.105
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 112 ---
The parallel flow is useful for speeding up workflows by handling independent
components at once. Imagine, for instance, instance, an AI system processing text,
images, and audio in parallel streams and then combining insights. This pattern is also
useful for exploring many possibilities concurrently , such as gener ating multiple solution
drafts at once.
Parallel flow is not suitable when sub-tasks have strong dependencies or must be done in
strict seque nce. Also, overhead can be high if results need heavy synchronization or
merging. If tasks are small or inherently serial, parallelization adds unnecessary complexit y
without much gain.
HIERARCHICAL COORDINATION
In this approach, a top-lev el manager or orchestr ator agent breaks a complex goal into
sub-tasks and assigns them to specializ ed worker agents. The manager then gathers and
integr ates the workers results. This creates a structured, top-down workflow; picture a a
boss agent o verseeing sev eral expert  agents.
Heirarchical coordinatio n is effectv e for complex, multi-step problems that benefit from
task decom position and specialization. For example, an orchestr ator agent can coordinate
multi-step research or content gener ation by dynamically deciding subtasks and picking the
best agent for each. It provides clear oversight and manages inter-dependencies among
sub-tasks.
This is overkill for simple or well-bounded tasks where a single agent suffices. The
coordination overhead and complexit y are high  if the leader agent fails or makes a poor
plan, the whole system  suffers. Heirarchical orchestr ation is not ideal when tasks cannot be
clearly decomposed, or when low latency is critical (due to planning o verhead).
ITERATIVE DEBATE AND REFINEMENT
In the iterative pattern, two or more agents iteratively propose solutions and critique each
other, refining the outcome through multiple rounds. Each agent brings a perspectiv e or
hypothesis; they discuss disagreements and improvements, and revise their answers based
on peer feedback. The process continues until they con verge on a well- vetted solution.
This iteration is valuable for high-stak es or ambiguous problems requiring thorough
analysis, verification, or creativit y. By leveraging diverse viewpoints, the agents can catch
each other s errors and impro ve reasoning. This strategy has been used to boost factual
accur acy and complex reasoning, as when agents debate a scientific question or policy
impact to reach a well-justified conclusion.
The approach is unsuita ble when quick, straightforward answers are needed or for trivial
tasks where the multi-r ound dialogue would be overkill. It can be slow and computationally
expensiv e since many LLM calls are involved. If agents share the same blind spots or
biases, debate may not help (they might simply reinforce each other), and reaching
consensus can sometimes a verage out creativ e solutions.106
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 113 ---
VOTING / BEST-OF-N (ENSEMBLE)
Here, several agents (or the same agent run multiple times) gener ate solutions for the
same problem, and then a voting or select ion mechanism picks the best answer or a
consensus result. This could be a simple majorit y vote, a weighted vote (giving more
weight to certain agents), or selection b y a judge agent.
Voting is useful for improving reliabilit y and accuracy by combining multiple opinions. Its
common in scenarios like diagnostic systems  or evaluations where multiple models and
agents assess an input, and the system choos es the majorit y or most consistent answer . It
reduces the impact of an y single agent s error or bias, since outliers can be out -voted.
But its less effectiv e if all agents are identical, as they might produce the same
mistak es, so you gain little by voting. And its inefficient when gener ating many answers is
costly or slow. If a task has a single correct output easily obtainable by one agent, then
running N instances and voting is overkill. Additionally , a nave majorit y vote can fail if the
majorit y is confidently wrong (due to a shared bias or misinformation).
ROLE-PLAYING COLLABORATION
In role-pla ying agents are assigned complem entary roles and interact in a cooper ative
dialog to accomplish a task. A classic example is the CAMEL framework s AI User and AI
Assistant  roles: one agent poses instructi ons or problems, and the other provides
solutions; the two work step-b y-step through the task. By simulating an instructor -assistant
(or client-developer , teacher -student) dynam ic, the agents can clarify requirements and
iteratively impro ve the output through their inter action.
Use this when a task benefits from an interactive exchange or multi-perspectiv e
brainstorming. For instance, in code gener ation, one agent can act as the client, 
describing requirements and testing code ,while another is the developer  writing the
code. Its also useful for gener ating conversational data or Q&A pairs, with one agent
asking questions and another answering. The role interpla y helps when the problem needs
iterative clarification or creativ e back -and-forth.
Role playing is not helpful if the roles dont actually add new information or value; for
simple tasks, a back-and-forth dialog just adds latency . There s also a risk of circular or
unproductiv e conversations if roles are poorly defined. Additionally , two agents might
amplif y each other s errors if they lack an external check (for example, a mistak en
assumption b y the user  agent could mislead the assistant ).
CONDITIONAL ROUTING (BRANCHING)
This system includes a router agent or logic that directs an input to one of several
specializ ed agents or workflows based on the inputs nature. Essentially , the first step is a
classifier deciding who should handle this?  then handing off to the appropriate expert
agent(s). For example, a user query might be analyz ed and then routed to a math problem-
solver agent vs. a legal-reasoning agent.107
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 114 ---
Conditional routing is ideal for heterogeneous task loads where not every agent should
attempt every query . They are commonly used in customer -support bots or assistant
frameworks that must handle multiple domains; the router can send technical questions to
a technical expert agent, or billing questions to a finance agent. This ensures each task is
handled b y the most qualified agent or chain.
This approach is not needed when all inputs  are similar or one gener alist agent can
handle them. Its also problematic if inputs are misclassified, as a wrong routing can lead to
a bad answer . If a query spans multiple domains (e.g. a question that is both technical and
financial), a simplistic router might struggle, so this strategy works best when tasks can be
cleanly separ ated. The routing process itself adds overhead, so its inefficient for simple,
uniform tasks.
PEER-TO-PEER NETWORK
In this approach, there is no central coordinator; agents communicate and cooper ate in a
decentr alized peer-to-peer manner . Each agent shares knowledge or partial results with
others, and the team converges on a solution collectiv ely. This emergent coordination can
involve broadcasting information or negotiation among agents instead of a top-down plan.
Peer to peer is especially useful for distributed decision-making and scenarios requiring
high robust ness. For example, in a cybersecurit y monitoring system, multiple detector
agents might exchange  alerts and insights to collectiv ely identif y threats without relying on
a single leader. Decentr alized setups can be more fault-toler ant (agents can pick up slack if
one fails) and adapt dynamically as agents join or lea ve.
Because no single agent oversees the process, this pattern is harder to design and
debug. So, its not advisable for tasks that need strict global control or a clear sequence of
actions, as a free-form network might suffer  from race conditions or inconsistent views).
Communication overhead can grow quickly as each agent interacts with many peers. If
agents do not have a shared strategy or conflict -resolution method, the system can drift or
deadlock. Like other complex plans, it is inefficient for small teams or straightforw ard
problems where a simple plan would suffice.
4.2 Balancing agents with agentic flows
The agent flow pattern  has evolved through the practical use of building production agent
systems. While we often prefer to use a single  agent, a flow of agents may usually be more
useful as a w ay to break down and understand the beha vior of y our agent's actions.
4.2.1 Transforming agents to agent flows
Unless you have some practical experience building agent systems, you will likely make
your first system using a single agent. For many simple use cases, a single agent is perfect.
Howev er, as we add functionalit y to an agent, this may require more tool use and, in turn,
require more complex instruction prompts. When agents become overloaded, you will often
find the need to break an agent into multiple agents, as shown in Figure 4.5.108
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 115 ---
Figure 4.5 A single agent is transformed into a multi-agent flow of agents. The agent role is broken down into
three well-defined and distinct roles that encapsulate a well-defined set of tools.109
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 116 ---
The typical transformation from a single agent to the agent -to-agent flow pattern is when
the single agent is broken up into multiple agents, each with specializ ed roles and
responsibilities to perform duties specific to their role in the flow. There may be various
reasons for tr ansforming single agents into a flow of agents, including:
Overload   the agent becomes o verloaded with numerous tools or long
instruction prompts and ma y fail to perform tasks (ex ecute tools)
correctly .    
Specialization   agents work better when highly focused on a specific
task or goal.     
Cost/latency  specializ ed agents, limited to a small set of tools and
relevant communication, are much cheaper to run than a single agent
with access to multiple tools and all communication. R emember , tools and
communication (context) represent additional tok ens that ma y only
provide limited or no v alue to each LLM ex change.

consumes the tools from multiple MCP servers and requires knowledge of using each, you
will notice in the code how the servers are created and wrapped in the same context. This
allows all three serv ers to be cleaned up after the agent completes its goal.110

4.2.2 Building an Agent-to-Agent flow
Agent -to-agent flows are similar to prompt chaining, in which we chain the output of one
prompt to the following  prompt. The difference between prompts and agents is the ability
to make decisions through tool use. In this section, we will convert the single agent
depicted in Figure 4.4 to the multi- agent flow pattern shown at the bottom of the figure.
Listing 4.2 shows the transformation of the single agent into an agent -to-agent flow. In
this example, we create a new agent for each MCP server and define a role specific to the
server. How you break up a single agent may vary based on the tools it uses, what roles
you want, and your expected outputs. As a gener al rule, think about specializing agents
first and then map roles appropriate to the specializations.#A Creates an MCP server from a Python file, this is the research source tools we used before.
#B A reference server used to assist agents in planning tasks for complex goals.
#C Filesystem reference server for interacting with the file system.
#D Instructions provided to the agent.
#E Wrap all three MCP servers in the same context.
#F Run the agent provided the goal.agent = Agent(
name="Assistant",
instructions=instructions,
mcp_servers=[research_srv, thinking_srv, fs_srv],
)
goal = """
Produce a research plan to find the book:
'The Hitchhiker's Guide to the Galaxy'
"""
print("Running...", goal)
result = await Runner.run(agent, goal)    #F
print(result.final_output)112

Ultimately , transforming the single agent to a flow allows you to add multiple agent steps,
MCP tools, and other output forms. It also allows you to control some of the decision-
making within the agent flow to make it more deterministic and predictable, as we will see
in the next section.
4.2.3 Agency and decision making in agent flows
The ability to make decisions and give agents  agency is a powerful tool. Howev er, because
LLMs power  agents, they are not determinist ic. This means command decisions within an
agent may vary based on several factors such as tool output, agent input, and so forth. To
make a flow or multi- agent system more deterministic, you may make those decision points
external and outside agent control, and instead controlled by process code to ensure
repeatable results.
Figure 4.6 shows an example of an agent flow with an external decision point
implemented by code. By coding this decision, we are sure that the output from the
Research agent is always above zero, effectiv ely constructing a guardr ail to prevent
unpredictable behavior. Leaving this decision to the agent may result in this hard rule
(guardr ail) being b ypassed.114
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 121 ---
Figure 4.6 shows an agent-to-agent flow with a deterministic (coded) decision point added. Taking the
decision away from the agents and making it deterministic keeps the workflow more consistent.
Listing 4.3 shows the changes added to suppo rt this deterministic decision-making process
(guardr ail) with the agent flow. As part of this update, we also updated the Research Tools
MCP server to produce  a random set of search sources. This change will simulate the
variabilit y of other tool use or processes.115
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 122 ---
We simulat e a random number of search sources to reflect an LLM-powered agent's
stochastic process and output. Remember , we always have to assume an output may vary
unless we take steps to make it more deterministic, through output typing or other
patterns. Listing 4.4 shows the changes to the agents and the agent flow needed to support
this decision making process.
A lot is going on in this code, so we will go through each section in some detail. First, we
update the Research Agent to return a constr ained list of strings as the search sources.
Doing this allows us to confirm more easily  when no sources are returned. If we just
allowed a string outpu t, we would have to write complex parsing logic to determine the
search sources.
Next, we define the decision point as the check that determines the number of search
sources. If no sources are returned, we default to a static string. When sources are
returned, we run the Thinking Agent to create the plan. But before doing this, we create a
dictionary to hold the inputs with a label. When converted to a string and sent to the agent,
this tr anslates to a JSON input with the same labels, which helps the agent.
Instead of passing the input as a dictionary , we could pass the conversation history of
each step. However, exchanging conversation history can lead to confusion for the agents,
and it is gener ally better to give an agent only the information it needs. This method also
significantly reduces the token overhead, but it still has limitations, which we will address in
this chapter .
After the decision point, we continue by passing the research plan (either real or static)
to the Filesystem Agent, which is then responsible for writing the file with the output.
Again, it is best practice always to output a consistent format and data type regardless of
internal
processes for agents and agent flows.Listing 4.3_variable_research_tools.py
#A Select a random number of sources to return
#B Return an empty list if no sources are returned.# variable research tools  updates
@mcp.tool()
def get_research_sources() -> list[str]:
"""Provides 0 to 3 random research sources."""
search_sources = [
"Wikipedia",
"Google",
"YouTube",
]
num_sources = random.randint(0, 3)    #A
if num_sources == 0:
return []    #B
return random.sample(search_sources, num_sources)116

 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 125 ---
Figure 4.7 three agent flow communication patterns demonstrating how agents can pass messages from one
another and in turn pass command and control from agent to agent.
Starting on the left side of the figure, we can see the conversational flow. This is an
established pattern where all agents commu nicate across the same conversation thread,
which includ es agent inputs/outputs, tool calls, and other noise. The benefit of this pattern
is that all agents share the same context. Howev er, this is also a detriment since it adds
overhead to each agent in the flow .119
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 126 ---
In the middle of the figure is an example of a pass-off communication system. Where
the code that calls the next agent controls the input and output of each agent call. We
already explored this pattern in the last section. The benefit to this pattern is complete
control over every agent interaction and the ability to interject controlled decisions.
Howev er, the difficult y is that everything needs to be handled manually in code, which can
significantly increase code complexit y.
Finally , on the left side of the figure is the enhanced agent handoff with a guardr ail
pattern that OpenAI Agents SDK emplo ys. The benefit here is that all communication can
be filtered and monit ored on the main conversation thread, maintaining context and
limiting token overhead . The disadv antage to this pattern is that restoring the conversation
thread requires some finesse if the handoff becomes brok en.
Various agent framewo rks will implement some form of the patterns in Figure 4.6,
including other hierarchical/orchestr ation structures we saw earlier . For now, though, we
will examine the con versational handoff pattern implemented b y OpenAI Agents.

to code the transition as we did earlier . Howev er, it does require that the agents themselv es

4.3.3 Monitoring the handoff
Currently , the default handling of the agent handoff does not indicate what data is handed
off to the next agent. Understanding what data is passed from agent to agent can be crucial
in debugging complex agent behavior that is not performing as expected. To expose and
see this data we need to emplo y the handoff  wrapper function to monitor what is passed
from one agent to the next and why the handoff was made. Listing 4.7 shows how this
function is used to monitor and potentially validate the data passed from one agent to
another .123
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 130 ---
Using the callback function, we can see the data as it passes from agent to agent.
Additionally , we could perform other data processing and execute other agent workflows at
the handoff  point. This may also seem like a good way to validate the data from agent to
agent, but in fact there is a better pattern for this, the guardr ail.
4.4 Validating agent flows with guardrails
To successfu lly create complex agent flows, we need to validate the data that enters and
exits a flow and the agent -to-agent transfers within a flow. Guardr ails offer this ability to
validate the data for agent flows using conversational handoffs, the pass-off pattern, or the
handoff callback we explored earlier . lets start by seeing how to implement the guardr ail
pattern defined b y the OpenAI Agents SDK.Listing 4.7_agent_to_agent_monitoring_handoffs.py
#A This is the function that will be called when the handoff is made
#B We set the input data type to the same as the agent exports, but it could be other data, such as the reason for 
the handoff.
#C Pass the handoff object wrapped around the agent that takes the handoffasync def research_handoff(    #A
ctx: RunContextWrapper[None],
sources: ResearchSourcesModel,    #B
):
print(f"Thinking agent called with sources: {sources.research_sources}")
research_agent.mcp_servers = [research_srv]
agent_handoff = handoff(    #C
agent=thinking_agent,
on_handoff=research_handoff,    #A
input_type=ResearchSourcesModel,    #B
)
research_agent.handoffs = [agent_handoff]    #C
thinking_agent.mcp_servers = [thinking_srv]
thinking_agent.handoffs = [filesystem_agent]
filesystem_agent.mcp_servers = [fs_srv]
print("Running...", goal)
result = await Runner.run(
research_agent,
goal,
max_turns=25,
)
print(result.final_output)124

4.4.1 Implementing input and output guardrails
We use guardr ails in softw are to control and limit what an application may do. Guardr ails
protect against errant processes or unfores een errors in traditional deterministic code
bases. They can monito r possible division by zero or more complex application outputs and
inputs.
In AI agent systems, which by nature produce variable output but are often fed highly
variable input, the risk of something failing increases. Guardr ails, the concept, is a way to
control not only the system's input and output but also what data flows from agent to
agent.
This example has a lot going on, so let's look at a high-lev el overview , as shown in
Figure 4.9.125
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 132 ---
Figure 4.9 Guardrails can be used to validate and control input and output of an agent.
The figure shows how the guardr ails now wrap the agent and can be used to validate or
correct the input. We could use code or agents to perform this validation. Agents,
particularly those powered with LLMs, allow us to easily parse and validate virtually any
input and output, as we will see in the next section.
Listing 4.8 is an exam ple of agent input and output guardr ails implemented with the
Guardr ail pattern exposed in the Agents SDK.126
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook

Listing 4.8_input_output_guardrails.py
class ResearchOutputModel(BaseModel):    #A
"""Output model for the research agent."""
research_plan: str
"""The final research plan as text."""
research_plan_file: str
"""The path to the research plan file."""
@input_guardrail    #B
async def research_guardrail(    #B
ctx: RunContextWrapper[None], 
agent: Agent, 
input: str | list[TResponseInputItem]
) -> GuardrailFunctionOutput:
forbidden_research = "The Hitchhiker's Guide to the Galaxy"
if forbidden_research in input:
research_forbidden = True
else:
research_forbidden = False
return GuardrailFunctionOutput(
output_info=f"user asked: {input}",
tripwire_triggered=research_forbidden,    #D
)
@output_guardrail    #C
async def research_output_guardrail(    #C
ctx: RunContextWrapper, 
agent: Agent, 
output: ResearchOutputModel    #A
) -> GuardrailFunctionOutput:
if len(output.research_plan) < 100:
insufficient_research = True
else:
insufficient_research = False
return GuardrailFunctionOutput(    
output_info="research plan length: {len(output.research_plan)}",
tripwire_triggered=insufficient_research,    #D
)

name="Assistant",127

4.6 Summary
Single- agent designs often hit scalabilit y walls; con verting a monolithic
agent into an agentic flow  (a chain of specialised agents) restores
clarity, extensibilit y, and performance.     
Agent -to-agent flows work lik e prompt -chaining with superpowers: each
node can in voke tools and reason independently y et pass concise, t yped
outputs downstream to k eep the context lean.     
Insert deterministic decision points  (code or schema checks)
wherev er the flow must repeat reliably; don t rely on stochastic LLM
judgment for pass/fail br anches.     
The OpenAI Agents SDK supports two hand-off st yles: con versational
(shared thread) and pass-off (explicit code routing). Choose
conversational for speed and pass-off for fine-gr ained control.     
Use the handoffs  field of the agent plus clear instructional prompts to
enable internal hand-offs  that require z ero extr a orchestr ation code.     
Visualise complex flows early using  draw_graph(),  and the Dashboard
Traces  view rev eals hidden loops, tool chains, and latency hotspots.     
Wrap risky tr ansfers in guardrails input/output v alidators that reject,
retry, or correct data before it corrupts the flow; tripwires surface as
explicit ex ceptions y ou can loop on.     
Guardr ails themselv es can be LLM-powered agents, giving y ou natur al-
language policies without brittle regex or length checksremember they ,
too, need schemas and tests.     
Tool limits still matter in multi- agent worlds: ev ery registered tool inflates
every call; k eep each agent s tool list tight (< 10) and scoped to its role
to avoid tok en bloat.     
Flow , orchestration , and manager-worker  are the three canonical
decision patterns. Start with plain flows and gr aduate to orchestr ators
only when centr alised delegation is truly required.     
Choose one communication la yer (shared memory , message passing,
MCP, or emerging A2A protocol) per project; mixing channels multiplies
debugging pain.     
A production-ready agentic flow blends t yped I/O , deterministic
checkpoints, visual tr aces, scoped tool sets, and guardr ails yielding
pipelines that can scale, reco ver, and ev olve without surprise.     139
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook

Sequential Flow , Parallel Delegation , Hierarchical Coordination ,

5  Agent Reaso ning
and Planning
This chapter covers
Ho w LLMs reason and plan 
Instructing agents t o reason and plan 
Adv anced planning with agents 
Utilizing the Sequential Thinking MCP ser ver
Reasoning, for an LLM, is the ability to break down a problem into tasks, and then use
planning to assemble an approach to carrying out those tasks Without the ability to reason,
agents become limited  in their ability to plan, and, ultimately , use agency to make
decisions, carry out actions, and achiev e a goal. As we will see, this core element of an
agent's abilit y often starts with the LLM powering it.
5.1 Understanding LLM Reasoning and Planning
We use our own reasoning all the time to achiev e complex goals or even simple ones, like
leaving home. For exam ple, to achiev e the goal of leaving home,  we may consider tasks
such as getting dressed, verifying that the toaster is off, and making sure to have our keys.
Internally , we create the plan: dress -> check toaster -> get keys -> leave. Then we
execute the plan according to the tasks.
Non-reasoning large language models are, by default, empirically token-prediction
engines. This means they dont have a thought process that enables them to reason
through and complete complex tasks. This limitation often restricts agents to performing
one task at a time.141
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 148 ---
Fortunately , we have developed prompt strategies to encour age the kind of reasoning
that can effectiv ely give agents the ability to break larger tasks down into smaller ones, and
create a plan for accomplishing them. Newer  foundational models, such as the o family
from OpenAI and Claude Sonnet, also incorpor ate this ability through model infrastructure
and training. Figure 5.1 compares the way an LLM with no reasoning (top) and a reasoning
model might approach a complex question.
Figure 5.1 compares differences to prompting strategies with reasoning and non-reasoning models142
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 149 ---
As you can see at the top, the interaction with typical non-reasoning models requires a goal
statement, plus a goal statement augmented with a reasoning and planning strategy . But a
reasoning model, at the bottom of the figure, emplo ys reasoning and planning on all
requests by default. It makes sense, then, to consider how well an LLM reasons, or if it
even reasons at all.
The obvious thought may always be to use LLMs that reason, and in fact, they are
almost standard today. Howev er, even when using LLMs that can reason and plan, you
often want to control how an agent or LLM reasons. The figure shows an example of
patterns we may use to interact with LLMs to activate reasoning, and well look at two of
those patter ns for encour aging an LLM, with or without reasoning, to reason. Both patterns,
Chain of Thought (CoT) and the Reasoning, Acting, Observing paradigm (ReAct) are tactical
or low-level reasoning, suitable for executing quick goals but not for more in-depth
planning They are practical techniques for ensuring that your agent applies some form of
reasoning and thought process regardless of the underlying LLM. This allows us to control
the agent's  thought process, which in turn becomes more reproducible and reduces
variabilit y.
5.1.1 Chain of Thought Reasoning
Chain-of -Thought (CoT) prompting guides an LLM to reason through a problem step-b y-step
instead of jumping straight to the answer . In a CoT approach, the model gener ates a
sequence of intermedia te reasoning steps (a chain of thought ) before producing the final
answer . This process effectiv ely lets the LLM solve complex tasks by breaking them down,
significantly impro ving performance on completing complex goals common to agents.
In CoT prompting, we provide the LLM with examples of how it should reason. We can
demonstr ate this easily by opening ChatGPT , selecting a non-reasoning or reasoning model,
and entering the example prompts in Listing 5.1.
There are nuances in this problem that make it difficult for a model without reasoning, and
even sometimes challenging for those who reason. The correct answer is 26 days, but you
may get answers from 6, 13, and 27 days depending on your chosen model. Some models
may even pro vide one answer one time and a different answer the next.Listing 5.1 01_demonstrating_CoT_reasoning.md
# base time travel problem
In a sci-fi film, Alex is a time traveler who decides to go back in time
to witness a famous historical event that took place 100 years ago,
which lasted for 10 days. He arrives three days before the event starts.
However, after spending six days in the past, he jumps forward in time
by 50 years and stays there for 20 days. Then, he travels back to
witness the end of the end. 
How many days does Alex spend in the past before he sees the end of the event?143
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 150 ---

To get the answer of 26 days we can calculate  the total as sum of the jumps, but to
make calculations more tangible let s assume a starting date 2026:
Jump 1: -100 years and 3 days before the event start (1926), spends 6 days in
1926
Jump 2: forw ard 50 y ears to 1976, still in Alex s past, spends 20 da ys in 1976
Jump 3: backw ard 50 years to the last day of the event in 1926, witness the event,
and assume no da ys spent in past
Jump 4: forw ard to 2026, present time
We put together the 6 days spent in 1926 with the 20 days in 1976 to get a total of
26 da ys spent in the past.

The LLM's difficult y with this problem is the inabilit y to alter viewpoints and references. We
can use a CoT prompt strategy to help the model reason by defining the thought process
within the prompt. Listing 5.2 shows the addition of the CoT reasoning that we are
instructing the LLM to use to solv e this t ype of problem.
Adding the reusable CoT thought process and steps to the prompt provides the LLM with
the steps to solve the problem. You will now see when you enter the full prompt, the
problem first, and then the CoT strategy , the LLM responds to each step with an output. In
most cases, adding the CoT will help provide the correct answer , but you may find that
some models will still struggle.
When a model struggles with this, dont assume the CoT is broken; instead,the steps
likely need to be adjusted to fit your specific model's grammar and style. Figure 5.2
compares the process an LLM will take to answer using no prompt reasoning or with CoT
reasoning.Listing 5.2 01_demonstrating_CoT_reasoning.md (continued)
#With reasoning - append to prompt
CoT Reasoning Strategy
Step 1: Define present day as Year 0, past as negative years.
Step 2: Map traveler's journey: where he goes and how long stays.
Step 3: Identify which time periods count as "in the past." from the travelers
perspective.
Step 4: Track days spent during each visit to past time periods.
Step 5: Add up all days spent in past before event ends.
Step 6: Verify calculation includes all past time, excludes present/future time.144
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 151 ---
Figure 5.2 compares the LLM thought process when using CoT prompting on the left and not using any
explicit reasoning instructions.
As the figure indicate s, when using CoT prompting, the LLM will output each of the
thoughts/steps in the undertaking. This can be very helpful in determining when a thought
process may have gone astray. It should also be noted that CoT is not a magic process; it
often requires consider able time to get an LLM to think the w ay you want it to .
The benefit of using a CoT prompting strategy is the ability to check the LLM's thought
process, ensure the LLM reasons and plans expectantly , and structure the thought process.
The disadv antages of using this pattern are the additional token usage, higher latency , and
additional output. Howev er, CoT has only gone so far in controlling the final production, and
as we will see in the next section, there are better methods to manage this.
5.1.2 ReAct Paradigm (Reasoning + Acting + Observing)
While CoT keeps the reasoning entirely internal, the ReAct paradigm allows an LLM to
alternate between reasoning and taking action s in an interactive loop. In ReAct, the model
gener ates thought steps, natur al language reasoning about what to do, and action steps,
which are commands to use an external tool or query an API, interlea ving them. After an
action, the model observes the result, then updates its reasoning based on that feedback,
and the cy cle repeats. Y ou can think of R eAct and CoT plus tools calls.
This thinking, doing, and observing synergy of ReAct lets the model dynamically gather
new information and adjust its plan. ReAct is an extension to CoT in that it uses the pattern
of creating thought steps to build its initial plan. Figure 5.3 demonstr ates the ReAct pattern
in a sequence diagr am showing the stages of reason, act, and observ e.145
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 152 ---
Figure 5.3 demonstrates the ReAct paradigm of reasoning in a sequential diagram. The agent thought
process occurs in the loop where the LLM first reasons, plans and then executes on the plan. After each
execution of a task (tool) the LLM observes the output and then reasons if it needs to continue looping or the
plan has been completed.
In the figure, a query is sent to an agent/assistant that begins a process loop. First, the
assistant reasons out the steps needed to answer the query . After building this
thought/step plan, the assistant executes a tool/MCP call and observ es the results. If the
assistant observes the goal as complete, the loop stops and outputs a final replay. Howev er,
if not completed, the assistant reasons on the current step plan and either continues with
the plan or creates a new thought/step plan and continues the loop .
This is the same process that many of the reasoning LLM models use to reason and solve
tasks. If the model shows this, you can observ e it by watching their thoughts, actions, and
observ ation process. We can take this beyond CoT and ReAct by incorpor ating planning as a
means coordinate an LLM's thoughts.146
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 153 ---
5.1.3 Planning with LLMs
Beyond single questions or tool calls, LLMs can engage in higher -level strategic planning to
tackle comp lex, multi-s tep goals. Planning means the model develops a strategic outline or
plan of action before or during the execution of thoughts/steps. The LLM/agent is planning
over a longer -horiz on and taking into account multiple CoT/R eAct ex ecution loops.
In contrast to bare CoT, which handles one query with a linear reasoning chain, or the
purely reactive loop of ReAct, deciding actions one thought step at a time. Planning allows
the model to maintain a global view of a problem and organiz e a sequence of sub-tasks to
achiev e an o verall objectiv e.
LLMs are very capable of building plans, provided a query or goal. They can execute on
that plan using tools and observ e the results, not unlike the ReAct paradigm we just
observ ed. Listing 5.3 demonstr ates the promp t we can use to ask ChatGPT/Claude/Gemini
to build us a plan giv en a desired goal.
Enter the query into any foundational model. You will likely get some activit y behind the
scenes, like web searc hes or other queries to find information and construct the plan.
Essentially , what we are doing here is engaging the LLM to build a plan, and in so doing, it
internally creates a plan to mak e the plan.
For example, we took this itinerary (plan) in San Francisco but realiz ed the
Explor atorium was closed on the first day, and no startups were available to tour. Given this
new information, we entered our itinerary with the updates into a foundational LLM and
asked it to update the plan. Listing 5.4 demonstr ates this process and the output the LLM
may respond with.Listing 5.3 01_planning_san_fran_trip.md
#A The query to generate the plan, use on any foundational LLM, enter this part to generate plan
#B Typical example response from the LLMUser:    #A
I have 3 days in San Francisco and love AI Agents. Can you plan my itinerary?
Provide three concise (10 words or less) summary bullet points, one for each day.
LLM:    #B
Day 1: Explore OpenAI HQ and AI exhibits at Exploratorium
Day 2: Visit Stanford AI Lab and Palo Alto innovation hubs
Day 3: Attend tech meetups, tour AI startups in SoMa147

similar . Given our observations/updates to the itinerary, the plan is now updated. We could
then ex ecute our itiner ary again, and if we encounter an y changes, ask for a new plan.
The entire process of planning, executing, and observing will be familiar as the ReAct
process, but we will be the ones executing each step. It also demonstr ates how we can

If we swap ourselv es with agents, we can see how an agent could apply these same

5.2 Instructing agents to reason and plan
The next step in understanding how reasoning and planning work is integr ating some
examples into agents. While LLMs power agents, they are also external to LLMs. This
dualit y allows them to build and ex ecute plans independently or as a team.

5.2.2 Implementing ReAct with Agents
The ReAct pattern is based on the use of planning, acting, and observ ation to complete a
goal. To do this, the LLM or agent must have access to tools so that it can use (act) those
tools (act) to observ e the output. Listing 5.6 shows an updated simple agent designed to
use R eAct, including the addition of a couple of tools.150
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook

Listing 5.6 02_ReAct_agent.py
#A Add a couple of tools for the agent to use/act with
#B Update the instructions to have the agent use the ReAct pattern
#C Create a complex time travel problem that requires tool use to solve
#D Output only shows the final answer and no step-by-step activity@function_tool    #A
def travel_back(year: int, years: int) -> int:
"""Travel back in time by a given number of years from the start year."""
return year - years
@function_tool    #A
def travel_forward(year: int, years: int) -> int:
"""Travel forward in time by a given number of years from the start year."""
return year + years
react_agent = Agent(
name="TimeTravelerReAct",
instructions=(    #B
"You are a time travel assistant. You have tools 'travel_back' and
'travel_forward' to perform time jumps. "
"First, think step-by-step about the problem. If needed, use the tools to
calculate dates. "
"After using a tool, reflect on the result and continue reasoning. "
"After gathering information, provide the final answer."
),
tools=[travel_back, travel_forward],
)

)    #C

react_agent,

5.3 Advanced reasoning with agents
Not all agents require reasoning; if they do, CoT or ReAct will cover most cases. Of course,
as you build more complex agentic systems that challenge more difficult goals, you need to
look into more specializ ed reasoning patterns. the Tree of Thought (ToT) and reflexion
reasoning str ategies..
5.3.1 Tree of Thought
Tree-of -Thoughts (ToT) is a reasoning framework that gener alizes the idea of chain-of -
thought by exploring multiple possible thought paths instead of a single linear chain.
Whereas CoT follows one train of thought, ToT allows the agent to branch out, consider
different approaches or hypotheses, and backtr ack when a line of reasoning seems
unpromising. The benefits of using ToT are the ability to think of and evaluate many
complex solutions to a problem. This approach is helpful for complex issues that benefit
from strategic explor ation and lookahead, such as planning tasks, puzzles, or games where
multiple mo ves should be examined
Figure 5.4 demonstr ates a first -step implementation of this pattern.152
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 159 ---
Figure 5.4 shows a partial workflow of the tree of thought process where thoughts are branched into nodes
and each node is executed to determine which is the best path to follow.
A lot is going on with the ToT pattern shown in the figure. It may help if we also look at the
process in a list of steps, that help decompose this complex reasoning str ategy:
1. The agent gener ates sev eral initial thoughts or possible solutions steps
for the giv en problem, forming the first lev el of a thought tree .    
2. For each candidate thought, the agent expands it b y thinking of the next
step(s), thereb y growing multiple br anches of reasoning in par allel.    
3. After expanding to a certain depth or number of steps, the agent (or a
heuristic) ev aluates the partial solutions at the leaf of each br anch. This
evaluation might estimate how lik ely that br anch will lead to a correct
solution. F or example, the model could be prompted to score each br anch
as promising or impossible to reach the goal.     
4. Based on these ev aluations, the algorithm prunes the low -potential
branches and selects the most promising br anch(es) to continue
expanding. The process then iter ates: expansion followed b y
evaluation/pruning.     153
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 160 ---
5. The search terminates when a satisfactory solution is found on one of the
branches or when a maximum search depth is reached, in which case the
best-scoring solution among the explored thoughts is chosen as the final
answer .    
We can adapt this whole process into an agent system, as shown in Listing 5.7. Howev er,
the listing only evaluates the initial thought step as a branch. We would have to iterate the
same pattern o ver each thought step for a complete solution.154
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook

)

generator, input=f"Problem: {problem}\nThink of a first step."

)

next_step = await Runner.run(    #E
generator, input=f"Current idea: {thought}\nNext step?"
)
promising_branches.append(f"{thought} ->
{next_step.final_output.strip()}")

"Expanded promising branch:",155

The listing shows only a partial implementation of the ToT reasoning pattern, and it is easy
to see how complex this approach can be. Furthermore, each thought step requires multiple
evaluations for each branch being considered. For even simple problems, this reasoning
strategy can consume man y tokens and tak e time to ev aluate each path.
5.3.2 Reflexion
Reflexion is an advanced strategy where the agent learns from its mistak es through self-
reflection. After each attempt to solve a problem, the agent evaluates the outcome and, if
the attempt was incorrect or suboptimal, gener ates reflectiv e feedback (a critique or hint)
and then tries again,  incorpor ating that feedback into its next attempt. In essence,
Reflexion turns the problem-solving session into an iterative loop of attempt   feedback 
revised attempt, which can be seen as a form of verbal self-reinforcement learning. It is a
more pliable approach than ToT, and is appropriate for difficult -to-solv e and ambiguous
problems.
Figure 5.5 shows the complete R eflexion str ategy as a flow chart.#A Create a ToT generator agent responsible for creating thoughts
#B Create a ToT evaluator agent responsible for evaluating thoughts
#C Generate a number of first step thoughts
#D Evaluate and expand each thought 
#E Expand this thought with a second steppromising_branches[0] if promising_branches else "None",
)156

In this example, we see a solver-critic agent flow where the solver agent attempts to solve
the problem , and the critic agent, knowing the answer , criticiz es and provides feedback
after every solution attempt. This feedback is fed back into the solver agent's instructions
through context that can be populated each time the agent is run using dynamic
instructions.
Ideally , using the Reflexion strategy for reasoning, planning, and evaluation through
feedback allows the base solver agent to improve over time. A significant shortcoming of
this pattern is the need to understand the correct solution to engage feedback. This is not
ideal, but if you have an extensiv e collection of similar problems, you can work through
solving a couple using Reflexion and then populate the feedback hints in memory/stor age
to solv e all of the issues in the collection.

5.4 Utilizing the Sequential Thinking MCP Server
Although the Sequentia l Thinking MCP server has several implementations, we will focus on
Anthropic's reference implementation. Your agents can use this server/tool as a reasoning
and planning scratchpad, which means the name, Sequential Thinking, can be a bit
confusing because the tool doesn t provide any thinking, but rather just a place to store
thoughts.
Allowing your agent a place to store thoughts lets your agent think more strategically .
Now, as the agent works through complex problems, it can create high-lev el strategic plans
and save them in the ST server. Then, as it works through each task and goal, it can review
and revise the global str ategic plan as needed.
5.4.1 Unchaining the Sequential Thinking Server
The Sequential Thinking Server exposes a single tool/function for the agent to use for
reasoning and planning. Before we even start developing an agent with this tool, or others
for that matter , it can be helpful to understand how it functions. Listing 5.9 is a basic
implementation that establishes an ST server and attaches an agent. This code outputs the
list of tools from the MCP serv er and the tools the agent can see/use.161

Listing 5.10 SequentialThinking tools/function description
A detailed tool for dynamic and reflective problem-solving through thoughts.
This tool helps analyze problems through a flexible thinking process that can
adapt and evolve.
Each thought can build on, question, or revise previous insights as understanding
deepens.
When to use this tool:
- Breaking down complex problems into steps
- Planning and design with room for revision
- Analysis that might need course correction
- Problems where the full scope might not be clear initially
- Problems that require a multi-step solution
- Tasks that need to maintain context over multiple steps
- Situations where irrelevant information needs to be filtered out
Key features:
- You can adjust total_thoughts up or down as you progress
- You can question or revise previous thoughts
- You can add more thoughts even after reaching what seemed like the end
- You can express uncertainty and explore alternative approaches
- Not every thought needs to build linearly - you can branch or backtrack
- Generates a solution hypothesis
- Verifies the hypothesis based on the Chain of Thought steps
- Repeats the process until satisfied
- Provides a correct answer
Parameters explained:
- thought: Your current thinking step, which can include:
* Regular analytical steps
* Revisions of previous thoughts
* Questions about previous decisions
* Realizations about needing more analysis
* Changes in approach
* Hypothesis generation
* Hypothesis verification
- next_thought_needed: True if you need more thinking, even if at what seemed
like the end
- thought_number: Current number in sequence (can go beyond initial total if
needed)
- total_thoughts: Current estimate of thoughts needed (can be adjusted up/down)
- is_revision: A boolean indicating if this thought revises previous thinking
- revises_thought: If is_revision is true, which thought number is being163
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 170 ---
It is important to remember that this tool description is what the agent sees and how it
interprets the tool's use. We should also note that this tool (SequentialThinking) does not
think or reason for itself, but is just a scratch pad and a thought tracker. Other
implementations of Sequential Thinking servers use LLMs and agents to reason and plan
thoughts.
You may also note that ST allows agents to use ReAct and ToT planning, reasoning, and
execution strategies. All you need to emplo y those strategies is the appropriate instructions
in your prompt.
5.4.2 Revisiting time travel problems with Sequential Thinking
To demonstrate how the Sequential Thinking server works, we will revisit solving complex
time travel problems from Listing 5.1. Listing  5.11 shows the updated code, including the
previous difficult time-tr avel problem, the updated agent instructions, and the addition of
the travel_back  and travel_forward  tools.
The code connects the MCP server and other tools it provides to an agent. Then it tells
the agent to solve this problem/prompt, which it does internally by using the tools provided
in code or through the MCP serv er.reconsidered
- branch_from_thought: If branching, which thought number is the branching point
- branch_id: Identifier for the current branch (if any)
- needs_more_thoughts: If reaching end but realizing more thoughts needed
You should:
1. Start with an initial estimate of needed thoughts, but be ready to adjust
2. Feel free to question or revise previous thoughts
3. Don't hesitate to add more thoughts if needed, even at the "end"
4. Express uncertainty when present
5. Mark thoughts that revise previous thinking or branch into new paths
6. Ignore information that is irrelevant to the current step
7. Generate a solution hypothesis when appropriate
8. Verify the hypothesis based on the Chain of Thought steps
9. Repeat the process until satisfied with the solution
10. Provide a single, ideally correct answer as the final output
11. Only set next_thought_needed to false when truly done and a satisfactory
answer is reached164

In the figure of the Traces page, we can see all the tool interactions the agent performs

we use this powerful tool to help us solv e complex problems and tasks.
5.4.3 Advanced reasoning with sequential thinking
The Sequential Thinking server allows you to combine different reasoning patterns and
helps you keep track of how each functions with the agent. For our last reasoning example,
we will combine all the reasoning patterns we have used in the chapter: CoT for planning,
ReAct and ToT for plan execution and evaluation, and Reflexion to provide feedback so that
the agent derives the right plan to solve the problem. Listing 5.12 shows how all these
reasoning strategies can be combined. Again , combining multiple reasoning patterns and
the use of tools like ST server are best left to complex, ambiguous tasks and not anything
requiring low latency and quick answers.
When you run this code example, expect it to take a while, and it will consume a few
tokens. This is because we have added both ToT and Reflexion reasoning strategies, which
are comput ational and token-hea vy consumers. The results of this agent will be very
different based on the underlying model you use to power the LLM. More advanced
reasoning models lik e will perform well, but others ma y not.167
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook

#A Add a new function for the agent to check in the answer is correct
#B The correct answer is 26 days
#C Update the agent instructions with added reasoning strategies
#D Tell the agent to branch its thoughts to employ ToT
#E Tell the agent to check its answer and if not correct continue reasoning, employ Reflexion@function_tool    #A
def how_correct_is_answer(days_in_the_past: int) -> str:
"""
Returns how many days away from the correct answer the provided answer is.
If the answer is correct, returns a confirmation message.
"""
correct_days = 26    #B
diff = days_in_the_past - correct_days
if diff == 0:
answer = "Correct answer!"
else:
answer = f"{diff:+d} days away from the correct answer."
print(f"Answer is {answer}")
return answer

First, think step-by-step about the problem to devise a plan.
You must use the tools to calculate dates. 
After using a tool, reflect on the result and continue reasoning. 
Consider braching out your reasoning into multiple branches if necessary.
Execute and consider each branch step-by-step.    #D
Check the answer is correct using 'how_correct_is_answer' tool.    #E
If the answer is correct (0 days) provide the final answer and plan.
If the answer is not correct, continue reasoning and try to find the correct
answer.    #E
"""168

Now that weve explored how reasoning and planning can create smarter agents, we will
look at how retriev al and context augmenta tion can be used to support knowledge and
memory in agents. We start with a look at the basis for retriev al and how we can search by
semantic meaning using  dense vector embeddings. Then we will look at the practical side of
using retriev al to build knowledge agents, agents that have access to external resources to
help answe r questions. Finally , we will emplo y retriev al to find relevant previous
experiences in the form of memory that will be used to help augment agent context.
6.1 Understanding retrieval in AI applications
Retriev al in agent and chat applications is a mechanism for obtaining knowledge/memories
stored in an externa l, long-liv ed storage system. Unstructured knowledge includes
conversation or task histories, facts, preferences, or other items necessary for
contextualizing a prompt. Structured knowledge, normally stored in databases or files, is
accessed through native functions or plugin s. Memories may consist of the previous
conversation thread, previous agent experiences, previous facts or preferences about the
user, or other experiential information.173

Memory and knowledge, as shown in Figure 6.1, are elements used to add further context
and relevant informati on to a system prompt or agent instructions. Agent and prompt
instructions can have the context augmented with everything from information about a
document to previous tasks or conversations and other related information. In both cases,
knowledge and memory context are obtain ed from external sources, such as vector
databases, document index es, databases and anywhere data can be stored and retriev ed
using search.
Figure 6.1 The process of retrieval for both memory and knowledge, and how it combines with augmentation.174
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 181 ---
Retriev al and augmentation are key elemen ts to exposing knowledge and memory to
agents. Retriev al is the process of searching for information provided some form of context,
typically the user's input. Augmentation is the process of providing retriev ed information as
additional context in a prompt to an LLM.
Knowledge is external information, such as documents, PDFs, database tables, and other
external sources. Likewise, memory is the structured/unstructured capture of experiences
that may provide additional context to help answer or achiev e a goal. Both knowledge and
memory data sources use retriev al as the basis for querying the unstructured or structured
information that lies within.
The combined retriev al and augmentation mechanism, called retriev al augmented
gener ation (RAG), has become a standard for providing relevant context to a prompt. The
exact mechanism that powers RAG also powers memory/knowledge, and its essential to
understand how it works.
6.1.1 The basics of retrieval augmented generation (RAG)
RAG has become a popular mechanism for supporting document chat or question- and-
answer chat. The system typically works by a user supplying a relevant document, such as
a PDF , and then using RAG and a large language model (LLM) to query the document.
Figure 6.2 shows how RAG can allow a document to be queried using an LLM. Before any
document can be queried, it must first be loaded, transformed into context chunks,
embedded into v ectors, and stored in a v ector database.175
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 182 ---
Figure 6.2 RAG can be broken up into two phases, ingestion and retrieval: first, documents are loaded,
transformed, embedded, and stored into a searchable datastore, and, second, they can be retrieved using
query mechanisms as part of context augmentation
A user can query previously ingested and indexed documents by submitting a query . That
query is then embedde d into a vector representation to search for similar chunks in the
vector database. Content similar to the query is then used as context and populated into
the prompt as augmen tation. The prompt is pushed to an LLM, which can use context
information to help answer the query .
For example , if the user queries, capital of France , that query is converted to a dense
vector representation using an embedding model. Next, that vector representation is used
to query a dense vector database looking for similar matches. When a match is found it is
returned (capital of France is Paris) and added/augmented to the prompt fed to the LLM.
The LLM can then use that context to answer the question correctly and grounded in
knowledge.176
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 183 ---
WHY DO AGENTS NEED KNOWLEDGE?
LLMs, by nature, are trained on vast sources of information that often include hard facts,
like the capital of France. Howev er, this internal knowledge of the model is fixed and static
during training. If the capital of France changes, the model will remain unaware of it and
still answer . To address this shortcoming and include more specializ ed and specific forms of
knowledge an agent may need, we use RAG to ground the agent in this new dynamic
knowledge.
Unstructured  memory/knowledge concepts often heavily rely on some format of text-
similarit y search following the retriev al patter n shown in figure 6.2. Figure 6.3 shows how
memory uses the same  embedding and vector database components. Rather than preload
documents, conversatio ns or parts of a conversation are embedded and saved to a vector
database.
Figure 6.3 Memory retrieval for augmented generation uses the same embedding patterns to index items to a
vector database.
The retrieval pattern and document indexing are nuanced and require careful consider ation
to be emplo yed success fully. This requires understanding how data is stored and retriev ed,
which well start to unfold in the next section.177
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 184 ---
6.1.2 Delving into semantic search and document indexing
Document indexing transforms a document s information to be semantically recovered. How
the index will be queried or searched also plays a factor , whether searching for a particular
set of words or w anting to match phr ase for phr ase.
A semantic  search is a search for content that matches the searched phrase by meaning.
The ability to search by meaning, semantically , is potent and eliminates the need to identif y
key words or other classic text search patterns.
With seman tic search, we can search for the concept/meaning rather than relying on
exact keywords or phrases. Take for example  the search term dog , if we were searching
for the word, we would only find documents that mention the word. Using a semantic
representation of dog allows us to match documents that may also mention dogs, but
also particular breeds of dogs, like a cockapoo or even documents on puppy care or other
related activities.
6.1.3 Applying vector similarity search
Lets look now at how a document can be transformed into a semantic vector,  or a
representation of text that can then be used to perform distance or similarit y matching.
There are numerous ways to convert text into a semantic vector, so well look at a simple
one.
Open the chapter_06  folder in a new Visual Studio Code (VS Code) workspace. Create a
new environment and pip install  the requirements.txt  file for all the chapter
dependencies. If y ou need help setting up a new Python en vironment, consult appendix A.
Now open the document_vector_similarity.py  file in VS Code, and review the top
section in Listing 6.1. This example uses Term Frequency Inverse Document Frequency
(TFIDF). This numerical statistic reflects how important a word is to a document in a
collection or set of documents by increasing proportionally to the number of times a word
appears in the document and offset by the frequency of the word in the document set. TF
IDF is a classic measure of understanding the importance of a document within a set of
documents.178
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 185 ---
Lets break down TFIDF into its two compon ents using the sample sentence, The sky is
blue and beautiful,  and focusing on the word blue.
TERM FREQUENCY (TF)
Term Frequency  measures how frequently a term occurs in a document. Because were
considering only a single document (our sample sentence), the simplest form of the TF for
blue can be calculated as the number of times blue appears in the document divided by the
total number of words in the document. Let s calculate it:
Number of times blue appears in the document: 1
    Total number of words in the document: 6
    TF = 1  6TF = .16
    
INVERSE DOCUMENT FREQUENCY (IDF)
Inverse Document Frequency  meas ures how importa nt a term is within the entire corpus.
Its calculat ed by dividing the total number of documents by the number of documents
containing the term and then taking the logarithm of that quotient:
IDF = log(Total number of documents  Number of documents containing the word)
    Listing 6.1 document_vector_similarity (transform to vector)
#A Samples of documents
#B Vectorization using TFIDF
#C Vectorize the documents.import plotly.graph_objects as go
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import TfidfVectorizer
from sklearn.metrics.pairwise import cosine_similarity
documents = [     #A
"The sky is blue and beautiful.",
"Love this blue and beautiful sky!",
"The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog.",
"A king's breakfast has sausages, ham, bacon, eggs, toast, and beans",
"I love green eggs, ham, sausages and bacon!",
"The brown fox is quick and the blue dog is lazy!",
"The sky is very blue and the sky is very beautiful today",
"The dog is lazy but the brown fox is quick!"
]
vectorizer = TfidfVectorizer()    #B
X = vectorizer.fit_transform(documents)   #C179
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 186 ---
In this example, the corpus is a small collection of eight documents, and blue appears in
four of these documents.
IDF = log(8  4)
    
TFIDF CALCULATION
Finally , the TFIDF score for blue in our samp le sentence is calculated by multip lying the TF
and the IDF scores:
TFIDF = TF  IDF
    
Lets compute the actual values for TFIDF for the word blue using the example provided;
first, the term frequency (how often the word occurs in the document) is computed as
follows:
TF = 1  6
    
Assuming the base of the logarithm is 10 (commonly used), the inverse document
frequency is computed as follows:
IDF = log10 (8  4)
    
Now lets calculate the exact TFIDF value for the word blue in the sentence, The sky is
blue and beautiful:
The Term Frequency (TF) is approximately 0.1670.
    The Inverse Document Frequency (IDF) is approximately 0.301.
    Thus, the TFIDF (TF  IDF) score for blue is approximately 0.050.
    
This TFIDF score indicates the relativ e impor tance of the word blue in the given document
(the sample sentence) within the context of the specified corpus (eight documents, with
blue appearing in four of them). Higher TF IDF scores imply greater importance.
We use TFIDF here because its simple to apply and understand. Now that we have the
elements represented as vectors, we can measure document similarit y using cosine
similarit y. Cosine similarit y is a measure used to calculate the cosine of the angle between
two nonzero vectors in a multidimensional space, indicating how similar they are,
irrespectiv e of their siz e.
Figure 6.4 shows how cosine distance compares the vector representations of two pieces
or documents of text. Cosine similarit y returns a value from 1 (not similar) to 1
(identical). Cosine distance  is a normaliz ed value ranging from 0 to 2, derived by taking 1
minus the cosine simila rity. A cosine distance  of 0 means identical items, and 2 indicates
complete opposites. In this example, the vectors are nonnegativ e and lie in [0, 1], so the
similarities will be between 0 and 1.180
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 187 ---
Figure 6.4 Cosine similarity search measures the angle/difference between vectors in any dimensional space

function from scikit-learn. Similarities are calculated for each document against all other
documents in the set. The computed matrix of similarities for documents is stored in the
cosine_similarities  variable. Then, in the input loop, the user can select the document
to view its similarities to the other documents.181
