6.2 Vector databases and similarity search
After vectorizing documents, they can be stored in a vector database for later similarit y
searches. To demonstr ate how this works, we can efficiently replicate a simple vector
database in Python code.
Open document_vector_database.py  in VS Code, as shown in listing 6.3. This code
demonstr ates creating a vector database in memory and then allowing users to enter text
to search the database and return results. The results returned show the document text
and the similarit y score.183
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 190 ---
Listing 6.3 document_vector_database.py
#A Stores the document vectors into an array
#B The function to perform similarity matching on query returns, matches,and similarity scores
#C The main input loop
#D Loops through results and outputs text and similarity score# code above omitted
vectorizer = TfidfVectorizer()
X = vectorizer.fit_transform(documents)
vector_database = X.toarray()    #A
def cosine_similarity_search(query,
database, 
vectorizer, 
top_n=5):    #B
query_vec = vectorizer.transform([query]).toarray()
similarities = cosine_similarity(query_vec, database)[0]
top_indices = np.argsort(-similarities)[:top_n]  # Top n indices
return [(idx, similarities[idx]) for idx in top_indices]
while True:     #C
query = input("Enter a search query (or 'exit' to stop): ")
if query.lower() == 'exit':
break
top_n = int(input("How many top matches do you want to see? "))
search_results = cosine_similarity_search(query,
vector_database, 
vectorizer, 
top_n)
print("Top Matched Documents:")
for idx, score in search_results:
print(f"- {documents[idx]} (Score: {score:.4f})")  #D
print("\n")
###Output

- Love this blue and beautiful sky! (Score: 0.3439)
- The brown fox is quick and the blue dog is lazy! (Score: 0.2560)184

Run this exercise to see the output (F5 in VS Code). Enter any text you like, and see the
results of documents being returned. This search form works well for matching words and
phrases with similar words and phrases, but it misses the word context and meaning from
the docume nt. So we need a way totransform documents into vectors that better preserv es
their semantic meaning.
6.2.1 Demystifying document embeddings
TFIDF is a simple form that tries to capture semantic meaning in documents. Howev er, its
unreliable because it only counts word frequency and doesn t understand the relationships
between words. A better and more modern method uses document embedding, a form of
document v ectorizing that better preserv es the semantic meaning of the document.
Embedding networks are constructed by training neural networks on large datasets to
map words,  sentences, or documents to high-dimensional vectors, capturing semantic and
syntactic relationships based on context and relationships in the data. You typically use a
pretrained model trained on massiv e datasets to embed documents and perform
embeddings. Models are available from many sources, including Hugging Face and, of
course, OpenAI.
In our next scenario , well use an OpenAI embedding model. These models are typically
perfect for capturing the semantic context of embedded documents. Listing 6.4 shows the
relevant code that uses OpenAI to embed the documents into vectors that are then reduced
to three dimensions and rendered into a plot.185
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 192 ---
When a document is embedded using an Open AI model, it transforms the text into a vector
with dimensions of 1536. We cant visualiz e this number of dimensions, so we use a
dimensionalit y reduction technique via principal component analysis (PCA) to convert the
vector of siz e 1536 to 3 dimensions.
Figure 6.6 shows the output gener ated from running the file in VS Code. By reducing the
embeddings to 3D, we can plot the output to show how semantically similar documents are
now grouped.Listing 6.4 document_visualizing_embeddings.py (relevant sections)
#A Join all the items on the string ', '.
#B Uses the OpenAI client to create the embedding
#C Generates embeddings for each document of size 1536 dimensions
#D Converts embeddings to a NumPy array for PCA
#E Applies PCA to reduce dimensions to 3 for plottingload_dotenv()                #A                                            
api_key = os.getenv('OPENAI_API_KEY')
if not api_key:
raise ValueError("No API key found. Please check your .env file.")
client = OpenAI(api_key=api_key)                                         #A
def get_embedding(text, model="text-embedding-ada-002"):    #B
text = text.replace("\n", " ")
return client.embeddings.create(input=[text],
model=model).data[0].embedding                #B
embeddings = [get_embedding(doc) for doc in documents]   #C
print(embeddings_array.shape)
embeddings_array = np.array(embeddings)   #D
pca = PCA(n_components=3)  #E
reduced_embeddings = pca.fit_transform(embeddings_array)186

You choose which embedding model or servic e to use. The OpenAI embedding models are

6.2.2 Querying document embeddings from Chroma
We can combine all the pieces and look at a complete example using a local vector
database called Chroma DB. Many vector database options exist, but Chroma DB is an
excellent local vector store for development or small-scale projects. There are also plenty of
more robust options that y ou can consider later .
Listing 6.5 shows the new and relevant code sections from the document_query_
chromadb.py  file. Note that the results are scored by distance and not by similarit y. Cosine
distance is determined b y this equation:
Cosine Distance(A,B) = 1  Cosine Similarity(A,B)
    
This means that cosine  distance will range from 0 for most similar to 2 for semantically
opposite in meaning.187
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook

Listing 6.5 document_query_chromadb.py (relevant code sections)
embeddings = [get_embedding(doc) for doc in documents]    #A
ids = [f"id{i}" for i in range(len(documents))]           #A
chroma_client = chromadb.Client()              #B
collection = chroma_client.create_collection(
name="documents")       #B
collection.add(    #C
embeddings=embeddings,
documents=documents,
ids=ids
)
def query_chromadb(query, top_n=2):     #D
query_embedding = get_embedding(query)
results = collection.query(
query_embeddings=[query_embedding],
n_results=top_n
)
return [(id, score, text) for id, score, text in
zip(results['ids'][0],
results['distances'][0], 
results['documents'][0])]
while True:    #E
query = input("Enter a search query (or 'exit' to stop): ")
if query.lower() == 'exit':
break
top_n = int(input("How many top matches do you want to see? "))
search_results = query_chromadb(query, top_n)
print("Top Matched Documents:")
for id, score, text in search_results:
print(f"""
ID:{id} TEXT: {text} SCORE: {round(score, 2)}
""")    #E
print("\n")

6.3 Building practical RAG knowledge agents
Similarit y or vector search provides an excellent foundation for building RAG systems.
Howev er, relying entirely on vector search is not a sufficient basis for a practical RAG
system. As we will see in the next section, we often use a combination of search
approaches when building RAG and RAG agents.
6.3.1 Everything begins with search and relevance
There are numerous approaches to finding information. Vector/similarit y search works well
because it gener ally returns documents with the same meaning. Then we feed all
documents with similar meanings into an LLM/Agent, which sorts out the relevant
information. This approach, howev er, can create a number of problems, Table 6.2 highlights
several common issues with using only a v ector/similarit y search for context retriev al..#A Generates embeddings for each document and assigns an ID

The important takeaways from Tables 6.1 and 6.2 are, first, that you gener ally will not rely
solely on vector search to power RAG. Next, when choosing a supplementary technique,
you always want to consider the type of data you use to power the RAG and the use cases.
Finally , don t rely entirely on the search mechanism to determine RAG context.
Agents add another layer to this because they can support a mix of tools to combine
these multiple patterns into even more compl ex RAG use cases. This allows an RAG agent
to search using one pattern, vector/h ybrid search, and then augment the relevance and
context of that search using a tool that performs a relational database search.
6.3.2 Building a vector search RAG agent
Perhaps the best way to understand all these terms and methods is to look at how they are
applied in code. Lets start by building a RAG engine powered solely by vector/similarit y
search. Well create a simple agent that will load a movie script, chunk it into sections,
embed those chunks into semantic vectors, and feed those vectors into a vector database
(chroma) for later retriev al using a search_script  tool. The result is a RAG agent that can
has knowledge of the particular mo vie script previously index ed/ingested.192
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 199 ---
The process  in detail, as you can see in listing 6.6, is to load the movie script in the
code, break it into semantic chunks by the number of tokens, and use the OpenAI
embedding model to create vectors. From there, we load the chroma database from a local
path and check if the collection exists. If the collection exists and there are no vectors, we
populate it; otherwise, we continue to the agent.
Next we provide the agent with a role and instructions to guide it, as well as a final
instruction to ensure all answers are grounded in the documents it finds. Grounding is like
telling the agent to make sure any answer it provides links back to the information it found
in the searc h. If we dont do this, the agent will cheat and try to use its internal knowledge,
which ma y or ma y not be accur ate.
In the listing we first ingest the movie script, Back to the Future into a semantic vector
database. Then after building the RAG movie script agent, we query two important details
from the script: Where does Doc tell Marty to meet him, and at what time?", and What
happens at 1:15AM? .193

Listing 6.6 02_RAG_agent_vectory.py
script_text =
Path("chapter_06/sample_documents/back_to_the_future.txt").read_text(
encoding="utf-8"
)
def simple_chunk(text, max_tokens=200):    #A
tokenizer = tiktoken.get_encoding("cl100k_base")
words, chunk, chunks = text.split(), [], []
for w in words:
if len(tokenizer.encode(" ".join(chunk + [w]))) > max_tokens:
chunks.append(" ".join(chunk))
chunk = [w]
else:
chunk.append(w)
if chunk:
chunks.append(" ".join(chunk))
return chunks
docs = simple_chunk(script_text, max_tokens=200)
client = chromadb.PersistentClient(    #B
path="./chapter_06/chroma_script_store"  
)
collection_name = "bttf_script"    
try:
collection = client.get_collection(collection_name)    #C
except Exception:    
collection = client.create_collection(name=collection_name)    #C
if collection.count() == 0:    #D
collection.add(ids=[str(uuid.uuid4()) for _ in docs], documents=docs)
@function_tool    #E
def search_script(query: str, top_k: int = 3) -> str:
res = collection.query(query_texts=[query], n_results=top_k)
if res and "documents" in res and res["documents"] and res["documents"][0]:
return "\n\n".join(res["documents"][0])
return "No relevant documents found."
agent = Agent(194

--- PAGE 202 ---
GR OUNDING
When developing RAG systems and agents , you almost always insist that the
LLM/Agent ground the answers it returns. When we say information or answers are
grounded that means they reference and align with the source document, knowledge
or memories. Not grounding an answer will often result in the agent providing an
answer based on its internal model, which is unsupported by the knowledge context.
This may occasionally lead to the correct answer , but in most cases, the wrong
answer or hallucinations will occur. Always make sure your agents use grounding to
provide the most accur ate answer .
After running the code, you will notice that the agent can faithfully answer the first
question, but because it is prompted to be grounded, it cannot answer the second question.
The reason it cannot answer the time question  is that vector search is not granular enough
to pick up on this key term. Instead, we need to combine our agent with a hybrid search,
vector, and k eyword search.
6.3.3 Building a hybrid search RAG agent
Many metho ds and tools provide hybrid searc h, nd most of them embed the hybrid search
mechanism within the tool. A vector and keyword search are run in tandem and scored by
relevance when a searc h is performed. Because keyword and vector search scores are not
interchangeable, reranking functions combin e the results to provide overall relevance.
Figure 6.7 demonstr ates how this approach works with search tools/pro viders and agents.196
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 203 ---
Figure 6.7 The top of figure shows how a user query could be converted to a hybrid search by decomposing the
query into keywords and semantic meaning, The bottom figure shows how an agent could use the same
search mechanism but as a set of tools to perform a semantic (vector) and keyword query.
At the top of the figure, we see a query passed into a hybrid search tool that uses vector
and keyword methods. After the search is performed, internally , the search service reranks
the documents based on a merged scoring mechanism called RRF (reciprocal rank fusion).
The results with the new score are returned.
When using  agents, we can alter how we apply hybrid search by allowing the agent to
use agency and decide when and how to use a vector and keyword search function. After
returning the documen ts from both functions, it is up to the agent to determine relevance
and answer the query appropriately , given the context.197
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook

Now, on top of ground ing the answer we also insist the agent provides references for

We now have an agent that can find information relevant to answering a question and

6.4 Adding memory to agents with MCP
RAG for knowledge or memory is a powerful concept that empowers agents to extend
beyond their fixed model. Howev er, as we have seen, it can be complex to implement these
systems effectively if you require anything beyond simple use cases. This is where MCP
really shines, as it can provide numerous tools out of the box to power agent knowledge
and memory . Before we get to that we want to understand what memory actually is in this
context.
6.4.1 Understanding memory form and agent function
Memory in agents and AI applications is often described in the same terms as cognitiv e
memory functions. Cognitive memory describes the type of memory we use to remember
what we did 30 seconds ago or how tall we were 30 years ago. Computer memory is also
an essential element of agent memory , but one we won t consider in this section.
Instead, well look at the memory that can be applied to AI agents. This memory is
broken down into three types, sensory , short-term, and long-term, each mapping to
particular agent functions.
Sensory memory in AI functions like RAG but with images/audio/haptic data forms. This
means the AI can sense (see, hear, and even feel) past experiences and remember them.
For example , a sensory memory may be an image that is retriev ed and augmented to an
agent if it sees similar images. Images may be converted to vector (meaning)
representations that can then be matched to similar documents.#A Explain to the agent how to use the search tools
#B Enforce the agent to provide references for each answer
#C Formatting the answer output, alternatively we could have use a strongly typed object to achieve the same 
results
#D Add the modified search tools to the agent200
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 207 ---
Short-term/working memory in AI acts as an active memory buffer of conversation
history . Were holding a limited amount of recent input and context for immediate analysis
and response gener ation. In agents and agent applications, short-term working memory is
the workspace the agent or agents use. This could be something as simple as the
conversation thread or a more complex blackboard workspace.
Long-term memory in AI is storage relevant to the agent s or users life. Semantic
memory provides a robust capacit y to store and retriev e relevant global or local facts and
concepts. This form of memory may go beyond semantic to include search mechanisms
such as k eyword, relational database, and gr aph databases.
You can see the memory t ypes brok en into finer detail in figure 6.8.
Figure 6.8 How memory is broken down into various forms, from sensory (visual, auditory, and touch) to short
term conversational memory and long-term episodic, semantic and implicit/procedural memories
While memory may use the same retriev al and augmentation mechanisms as knowledge, it
typically differs significantly when updating or appending memories. Figure 6.9
demonstr ates the process of capturing and using semantic memories to augment prompts.
Because memories are often different in form and size of complete documents, we may
avoid using  splitting or chunking mechanis ms. Howev er, for more complex episodic,
semantic and procedur al memories may be broken (chunk ed) by events or other
boundaries.201
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 208 ---
Figure 6.9 Basic semantic memory retrieval and augmentation workflow. At the top of the figure memories
are remembered by being semantically encoded in a vector database. Bottom of figure we can see the basic
RAG process of retrieving and augmenting agent context.
The figure shows a semantic memory process, but the same principles apply to other
storage/retriev al forms we may use. Also, much like the knowledge search patterns we
looked at earlier , the form and types of memory we use will depend on what we want to
remember .
A keyword or relationa l database-back ed memory may be more appropriate if we want
to remember facts and statistics. Whereas, if we wanted to remember important
relationships about social networks, or facts and relationships about our person, we may
instead w ant to use a gr aph database.202
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 209 ---

When running this code, you can now converse with the agent, and it will remember
various facts, relationships, and observ ations. Using a graph database makes it more
efficient to remember facts, but it also has limitations.
For example, if we started talking about Doc as an entity and discussed various
observ ations, the agent would create an entity called Doc and add observ ations about it.
Howev er, perhaps the next time we talked to the agent, we mentioned Doc Brown, and the
agent ma y rightly assume this is a new entit y.
The fix for this is to create a hybrid memory solution that stores and queries information
using multiple forms of search/retriev al, for instance a hybrid memory agent that uses both
graph and semantic memory .
6.4.3 Creating hybrid memory systems with MCP
For the following example, we will use MCP servers for graph and semantic memory . That
means that all the work we need to do to get the agent to use both for memory is in the
agent instru ctions. Listing 6.9 shows the instructions we provide to the hybrid memory
agent to use both gr aph and semantic stores for memory .207

Listing 6.9 04_hybrid_memory_agent.py (agent instructions only)
You are a Hybrid Memory Management Agent that combines semantic memory (ChromaDB
vector store) 
with graph-based memory (knowledge graph) to provide comprehensive memory
capabilities.
CRITICAL: You MUST start EVERY conversation by saying "Remembering..."
and then execute the hybrid memory retrieval workflow.
HYBRID MEMORY SYSTEM:
1. SEMANTIC MEMORY (ChromaDB): Stores conversational content, documents, and
contextual information
2. GRAPH MEMORY (Knowledge Graph): Stores structured facts, relationships,
entities, and observations
MANDATORY HYBRID WORKFLOW FOR EVERY INTERACTION:
Remembering -> Semantic Search -> Graph Search -> Hybrid synthesis -> Memory
capture -> Monitor -> Response
1. ALWAYS START WITH: "Remembering..."
2. SEMANTIC MEMORY SEARCH (FIRST STEP):
- Use chroma_query_documents to search for relevant conversational content
- Query for topics, concepts, or keywords related to the user's input
- This provides contextual understanding and conversation history

* Entities mentioned in semantic results (search_nodes)
* Relationships between entities (search_relations) 
* Specific observations and facts (search_observations)
- Focus on default_user and related entities
4. HYBRID RESPONSE SYNTHESIS:
- Combine semantic context with structured graph knowledge
- Reference both conversational context AND structured facts
- Provide rich, contextual responses using both memory types
5. INFORMATION CAPTURE & STORAGE:
When new information is discovered, store in BOTH systems:
a) SEMANTIC STORAGE (ChromaDB):
- Store full conversational content and context208

3. Agent interprets entities and observ ations from the returned semantic

Combining these search/retriev al patterns makes our agents' memory more robust in
finding relevant information. Of course, we could continue to extend this memory by adding
keywords or even relational database memories. How far you extend this memory will
depend on your use case and what the agent or agents need to do. Now lets turn to more
unique and fundamental aspects of memory .
6.4.4 Semantic augmented memory and applications to semantic, episodic, and
procedural memory
Psychologists categoriz e memory into multiple forms, depending on what information is
remembered. Semantic, episodic, and procedur al memory all represent different types of
information. Episodic  memories are about events, procedural  memories are about the
process or steps, and semantic  memories represent the meaning and could include feelings
or emotions.
Episodic and procedur al memories fit well within the relational database search/retriev al
pattern, where you can record events (episodic) and steps or processes (procedur al).
Howev er, like other hybrid forms, you may also want to record these memories in
semantic/v ector storage for more gener al retrieval. Essentially , what we do with these
types of memories is augment them with additional semantic meaning. Figure 6.11 shows
the semant ic memory augmentation process that could be applied to episodic and
procedur al memories.210
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 217 ---
Figure 6.11 Semantic memory augmentation works by feeding previous experiences into an LLM that then
converts the experience into an augmented memory of questions a user may ask that will activate that
memory in a future retrieval step.
Memory augmentation gener ates additional questions relating to the query/statement,
event, or procedur al step. Essentially , we want to link these memories to the questions or
statements a user may make to find them. You can think of this as reverse engineering the
meaning and projecting what questions a user ma y ask to retriev e these memories..
6.4.5 Uncluttering memory with compression and forgetting
Much like our memory , memory stores can become cluttered with redundant information
and numerous unrelated details over time. Internally , our minds deal with memory clutter
by compressing or summarizing memories. Our minds remember more significant details
over less important ones, and memories accessed more frequently .
We can apply similar principles of memory compression to agent memory and other
retriev al systems to extract significant details. The principle of compression is identical to
semantic augmentation but adds another layer that collects memories into sets and then
summariz es the memory set into a single memory. This pattern works best with semantic
memory; there is no reason it could not be applied to graph knowledge, keywords, or
relational databases.211
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 218 ---
Figure 6.12 shows the process of memory/knowledge compression. Memories or
knowledge are first clustered using an algorithm such as k-means. Then, the groups of
memories are passed through a compression function, which summariz es and collects the
items into more succinct representations.
Figure 6.12 Memory and knowledge compression works by semantically clustering similar
memories/knowledge into groups, it then summarizes these groups into a new single memory for each group.
Then each single group memory is entered into an updated vector database..
Compressing memories  and even knowledge is gener ally recommended if the number of
items in a cluster is large or unbalanced. Each use case for compression may vary
depending on the use and application of memories. Gener ally, though, if an inspection of
the items in a store contains repetitiv e or duplicate information, its a good time for
compression. The following is a summary of use cases for applications that would benefit
from compression.
THE CASE FOR KNOWLEDGE COMPRESSION
Knowledge retriev al and augmentation have also been shown to benefit significantly from
compression. Results will vary by use case, but gener ally, the more verbose the source of
knowledge, the more it will benefit from compression. Documents that feature literary
prose, such as stories and novels, will benefit more than, say, a base of code. Howev er, if
the code is lik ewise v ery repetitiv e, compression could also be shown to be beneficial.
THE CASE FOR HOW OFTEN YOU APPLY COMPRESSION
Memory will often benefit from periodic compression, whereas knowledge stores typically
only help on the first load. How frequently you apply compression will greatly depend on
the memory use, frequency , and quantit y.212
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 219 ---
THE CASE FOR APPLYING COMPRESSION MORE THAN ONCE
Multiple passes of compression simultaneously have been shown to impro ve retriev al
performance. Other patterns have also sugge sted using memory or knowledge at various
compression levels. For example, a knowledge store is compressed two times, resulting in
three different lev els of expertise.
THE CASE FOR BLENDING KNOWLEDGE AND MEMORY COMPRESSION
If a system is specializ ed to a particular source of knowledge and emplo ys memories, there
may be further optimization to consolidate stores. Another approach is to populate memory
directly with the starting knowledge of a document.
THE CASE FOR MULTIPLE MEMORY OR KNOWLEDGE STORES
In more advanced systems, well examine agents emplo ying multiple memory and
knowledge stores relevant to their workflow . For example, an agent could emplo y individual
memory stores in its conversations with individual users, perhaps including the ability to
share different groups of memory with different groups of individuals. Memory and
knowledge retriev al are cornerstones of agentic systems, and we can now summariz e what
we co vered and review some learning ex ercises in the next section.
THE CASE FOR FORGETTING
Compression is one strategy for reducing and simplif ying multiple replicated memories.
Another approach is to borrow another facet of human memory , forgetting. By introducing
forgetting, we allow memory pruning of old, likely outdated knowledge and replace it with
new knowledge/memories. The benefit here is that the store is staying the same over time,
and old, usually redun dant knowledge is getting replaced. Unlike compression, howev er,
forgetting/pruning memories remo ves all tr ace.
Whether you decide to apply memory compression or forgetting depends on your use
case and the informatio n you need your agent to remember . If an agent's memory is filled
with multipl e repetitiv e memories, forgetting can be a beneficial practice. Compression is
better suited if y ou ha ve numerous similar memories, with minor differences.
As you may realiz e by now, giving your agents knowledge and memory provides
powerful capabilities. Howev er, these tools are complex and may require additional
integr ation with other patterns for the best beha vior.

6.6 Summary
Retriev al is the bridge between r aw data and an agent s reasoning: RAG
pipelines turn external text (or other modalities) into prompt -ready
context, enabling agents to answer questions with up-to-date, task -
specific knowledge.     
Vector similarit y search is the semantic workhorse, but b y itself it misses
exact matches, jargon, numbers, and fresh updatesplan to combine it
with k eyword, relational, or gr aph queries for reliable co verage.    
Vector databases (e.g. , Chroma, Pinecone) store high-dimensional
embeddings and let agents run millisecond-scale similarit y look -ups; they
are essential infr astructure for an y production RAG or memory system.     
Hybrid search (v ector + k eyword) offers the best of both worlds: lexical
precision and semantic recall. R eciprocal-r ank fusion or agent -directed
tool chaining merges the two result sets into a single, r anked context.     215
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 222 ---
Typed tool interfaces (via the OpenAI Agents SDK or MCP) let agents
decide when to call semantic, k eyword, SQL, or gr aph toolsgiving them
true agency while k eeping each search cost -controlled and observ able.    
Memory and knowledge are siblings, not twins: knowledge is usually
static document context, while memory ev olves from con versations and
agent actions; both ride on the same retriev al primitiv es.    
Cognitiv e analogues map cleanly to implementation la yers: sensory
memory  short -lived embeddings, working memory  con versation
buffer or blackboard, long-term memory  v ector/gr aph/SQL stores.     
Graph memory adds relational reasoningentities, edges, and
observ ationsso an agent can answer multi-hop questions (Who reports
to Alice s manager?) that pure embeddings cannot resolv e.    
MCP serv ers abstr act memory back -ends: plug in Chroma for semantic
recall, a knowledge-gr aph serv er for relationships, or both for a h ybrid
memory agent without rewriting core agent code.     
Compression and forgetting k eep stores lean: cluster similar chunks and
summarise them, or prune stale items, to control tok en costs and prev ent
retriev al noise as memories grow .    
Grounding is non-negotiable always require agents to cite retriev ed
passages; it prev ents hallucinations and pins answers to v erifiable
sources.     
In practice, production-gr ade knowledge/memory agents mix multiple
retriev al modes, strongly t ype their tool calls, tr ace ev ery inter action, and
apply periodic compression yielding context -aware systems that sta y
accur ate, efficient, and scalable o ver time.     216

Building robust, reliabl e, safe and debuggabl e agentic systems is all about implementing
evaluation and feedback. Agent evaluation comes in many forms, from benchmark testing,
red team testing, grounding, and even agents that evaluate agents. Likewise, feedback
systems developed for agents may come from human experience, agent evaluators or
critics, testing output, and self -assessment.
While it is gener ally a requirement to implement evaluation and feedback into any
production agent system, this shouldn t be the only time you look at hardening your agent
systems. You almost always want to roll in this final layer (Layer 5 - Evaluation and
Feedback), which we will explore in this chapter .217

7.1 Introducing agent evaluation and feedback
In this section, we zoom out and map the different evaluations and feedback loops around
an agent (tests, human s, automated evaluators). Agents rely on an internal evaluation and
feedback mechanism that is activated in the Learn step of the Sense-Plan- Act-Learn
process loop. In addition, we can provide external mechanisms that evaluate and provide
feedback to the agent or to ourselv es. Figure 7.1 highlights the various steps and systems
that may be used for agent evaluation and feedback: red-team and benchmark testing,
ground or critic agent ev aluators, and human ev aluation.
Figure 7.1 highlights the external agent evaluation and feedback systems for a single user-facing agent. In
the figure a generative agent may face evaluation from a critic for general output feedback or a grounding
agent for context specific feedback and evalution.218
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 225 ---
Each of these evaluatio ns can then provide feedback, either directly to the agent from the
critic and grounding agents, or stored in an evaluation/feedback database that captures
test results  and human feedback. Evaluation may be implemented as deterministic test
cases for structured output evaluation, determining correctness or regression. Using agents
or LLMs for more fuzzy natur al language evaluation of classification, rubric scoring, and so
on. To human users who ma y encounter edge cases, policy content issues, or tone issues.
From this database, we can attach a specialized feedback agent whose role is to
constantly review evaluations and feedback to gener ate reports and dashboards or
gener ate alerts if some activit y becomes critical. While not all systems require all forms of
evaluation/feedback, you gener ally want to emplo y the following methods when
appropriate:
Red team testing   This ev aluates whether specific terms, phr ases, or
patterns will break y our system. An y user -facing agent or agent system
should undergo some form of red team testing.     
Benchmark testing  Tests how well an agent solv es or attains a known
problem or goal. These t ypes of comprehensiv e tests are intended to
cover entire agent systems, but could also be used to ev aluate individual
agents.     
Human feedback   An y user -facing agent system should pro vide a
mechanism to allow the user to pro vide feedback (thumbs up/down) and
comments. This feedback can be critical to identif ying problems early or if
other odd beha viors creep in. Howev er, it is essential to understand that
users are subject to error and bias, and all such feedback should be
verified.     
Grounding agent  This agent ev aluates RAG knowledge agents to
determine if a response has been grounded within the context the
knowledge agent w as giv en. An y system dependent on RAG agents
should emplo y a grounding agent that confirms all output has been
grounded in the knowledge base, thus prev enting hallucinations or
inappropriate responses.     
Evaluation/critic agent  This agent is used to pro vide an additional
level of ev aluation and feedback for internal reasoning when planning,
determining goal completion, and understanding action output. While this
form of agent ma y be added to an y agent workflow , it is commonly
emplo yed in the flow and collabor ation multi- agent patterns.     
You may encounter other evaluation and feedback patterns that are derivations or
extensions of these methods. Again, dont wait until the later stages of development to
emplo y evaluation and feedback. Much like we use testing to drive patterns like TDD (test-
driven dev elopment), consider utilizing the same pr actices when building agents.219
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 226 ---
7.2 Implementing test-driven agent development
TDD or test-driven development is an established softw are engineering practice that
requires developers to think and build tests first, before writing code. There is no reason we
cant and shouldn t emplo y such methods to build robust and capable agent systems.
Figure 7.2 demonstr ates TDD, which, when it applies to building agents, is known as TDAD
or test -driven agent dev elopment.
Figure 7.2 highlights the human process of developing agents using TDAD. Where we first think of goals and
benchmarks, then test, develop the prompt, refactor, and then repeat the process.
The figure demonstr ates the human process of building an agent (prompt and tools) by
undertaking the TDAD process, which starts with Thinking, Testing, Prompting, and
Refactoring. In TDAD , we first think about establishing a set of goals or input benchmarks
against which the agent will be tested. Within each benchmark, we identif y the expected
agent response and include unexpected or incorrect responses. Examples of incorrect
output can help an evaluation agent better define the differences between right and wrong
output.220
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 227 ---
Then we test, and if this is the start of testing, we should expect the agent to fail.
Because LLMs that power agents are variable, you will often want to run multiple instances
of the same test to confirm consistent agent behavior. From this failure, we then want to
determine the minimum  updates to the agents' prompt (instructions) and tools that will
allow the benchmark to pass. By doing the minimum, we keep the agent instructions
uncomplicated and lean, which helps maintain performance and impro ve latency .
Next, we retest and refactor . We re-run all previous passing benchmarks to confirm the
agent is working as expected. When previous benchmarks break, we go back and refactor
the prompt, making minimal changes to allow all benchmarks to pass. Continue refactoring
and testing to confirm the agent passes all benchmarks optimally .
We will gener ally want the agent to pass all benchmarks before continuing. Howev er, it is
not out of the ordinary that you may find previous benchmarks failing because of changes
to support other benchmarks. This will likely be because one benchmark may contradict
another . For example, one benchmark may force an agent to think step by step, while
another may require it to answer quickly without thinking. In such cases where benchmarks
start to break down, y ou ma y want to consider the following questions and options:
Should all benchmarks pass?   If the answer is no , then use the
failing benchmarks as an example of wrong output. Another reason this
answer ma y be no is that y ou only expect the agent to perform well on
90% of y our established tests. This ma y be because y ou ha ve doz ens or
hundreds of benchmarks dev eloped o ver time, and it is not alw ays
practical to achiev e 100% passing.     
Does adding an agent make sense?   If y our agent is passing most
benchmarks but failing on a subset, it ma y mak e sense to create another
agent that handles just those benchmarks. Howev er, you need to
consider how multiple agents ma y answer a goal benchmark. This ma y
require adding triage agents or emplo ying a mixture of expert answering
methodologies. Adding these additional agents and patterns increases the
workload, so it ma y mak e more sense to refactor the whole agent.     
Should you refactor your entire agent?   Adding agents is a lot more
work, and often it ma y just mak e sense to break the agent down and
rebuild it. F ortunately , rebuilding an agent often only in volves recreating
the agent's instruction prompt from the beginning. This time, when y ou
rebuild the agents, the instructions focus on the benchmarks that caused
the most issues or are the most complex.     
Gener ally, you will want to apply TDAD across an agent system and focus on individual
specializ ed agents where most of the work is done. Doing this will require agent and agent
system benchmarks that can be applied and tested for different granularities. But if you
dont do this, you risk potential gaps in functionalit y that could be uncovered during
production use.221
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 228 ---
7.2.1 Exploring TDAD in practice
Lets go through the process of creating an agent with TDAD . This will demonstr ate the
process so that you can use it yourself in building a single or multi- agent system. Figure
7.3 present s a single- agent system and some initial benchmarks we would expect this
agent to achiev e. In this example, our benchmarks consist of a set of questions specific to
some fictional example source material.
Figure 7.3 Architecture of a simple RAG Knowledge Agent. The goal of the agent is to answer questions from a
benchmark by searching its knowledge base and returning answers. A simple evaluator LLM evaluates the
answer based on the expected and wrong answers from the benchmark test. The accurary of the RAG agent
can be determined by the percentage of correct answers answered from the benchmark.
In the figure, the RAG agent relies on a simple retriev al architecture pattern. In this
pattern, an agent receives a question about  some external knowledge it has access to.
From there, it breaks down the question into searchable words and phrases and then uses
those terms to search its knowledge base. If knowledge is found, it uses that as context to
answer the user's question; otherwise, it returns that nothing was found. This answer is
then passed to an evaluator , who is also given the expected and incorrect answer
candidates paired with the benchmark who then uses this information to determine if the
benchmark passed or failed.222
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 229 ---
7.2.2 Coding and testing the RAG agent
Now that we have an idea of the benchmarks, our agent should be evaluated as we move
on to buildin g the minimum code to support this. Our focus at this point is to do everything
so that we can ask the agent a question, and it will answer , and that answer can be
evaluated. How you build this code is up to you, and you may want to use TDD for the
regular softw are pieces, lik e database connectivit y and other tools.
Listing 7.1 shows the code that will run the agent through the benchmarks table. At this
stage, the evaluation is simple and uses string matching against the expected answer . For
the agent itself, all we have defined is the role/persona in the instructions and nothing else.
Remember , our goal with TDAD is to build the minimal set of instructions with the minimum
set of tools to pass all our benchmark goals.223

@function_tool    #C
def search_knowledge(query: str) -> dict:
"""Search the knowledge database for relevant facts."""
matches = [doc for doc in _special_knowledge_db if query.lower() in

return {"status": "ok", "context": "\n".join(matches)}

You are a retrieval-augmented knowledge agent.

Having to run benchma rks multiple times contrasts with TDD, where one pass is all you

What we did in the listing is ensure that we always output single- word answers, which will
match our benchmark formatting. Next, we provide some instructions on breaking down
the question, but for now, we avoid specifically instructing tool use. As a guide, we want to
avoid formatting and tool use instructions in the prompt.
AGENT PROMPT DESIGN RULE
In the early days of agent development, we often explicitly called out how to specifically
use tools or format output. In today's agents, we strongly avoid even mentioning specific
tools or any formatting output guidelines. This is because we want the agent to dynamically
discover the tool format and output structure from the metadata. Think of this dynamic
discovery as something akin to code or modu le injection in softw are. By keeping the tool
use and output format out of the prompt are agents become more portable and less
constricted.
Formatting and tool use instructions should be provided outside the prompt. For
formatting, this means setting the output type on the agent, and then from this strongly
typed output format, construct the output you want. As for tools, the tool description and
the comment at the head of the function should provide the explanation the agent requires
to use the tool.
Keeping formatting and tool use outside the agent instruction prompt builds patterns
that can easily be adapted to other tools and outputs. Remember , anytime you manipulate
an agent's instructions, you risk the agent behaving differently . This goes for small or large
changes.

7.3 Employing grounding, critic, and evaluation agents
When evaluating the output of an agent, we have a few options and patterns, including the
grounding agent, critic agents, and evaluation agents. These three patterns help establish
practices we can use to evaluate an agent's output specific to an application or use case,
like knowledge RAG. Figure 7.4 compares the three patterns and the use cases they apply
to best and when to use them.
Figure 7.4 Three forms of agents for evaluating agent output: grunding, critic, and evaluation. The grounding
is specific to evaluating RAG agent output. The critic agent is designed to critique generated content, and the
evaluation agent is used for more complex output.
The grounding agent is specific to the RAG or retriev al agent and is used to make sure the
output gener ated is grounded in the contex t the agent used to create the answer . A
grounding agent may provide feedback and force the retriev al agent to regener ate or block
the answer entirely . This agent is essential when the answer provided needs to be grounded
in the context of the source material.231
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 238 ---
Critics are used to evaluate purely gener ated content and provide feedback when the
content doesn t fulfill the criteria or rubrics. Critics will block content because of a failed
evaluation and require regener ation. To avoid continuous feedback, the critic may allow
content after three attempts, for example.
For more complex output that may combine  retriev al and novel gener ation, we use
rubrics to help align content by providing evaluation as feedback during gener ation.
Because this process may be expensiv e to determine the cause of failures, the
feedback/ev aluation is sometimes combined with the complex output. This allows users to
understand and ev aluate the output themselv es and adjust accordingly .
7.3.1 Reviewing the grounding agent
As a review , grounding  is the process of making sure an answer is supported by context
and citation s. The practice of grounding isnt limited to RAG knowledge agents, but the
concept can be applied to other patterns like agent critics. Figure 7.5 revisits the
architecture of the RAG knowledge agent, now with a grounding guardr ail agent. Adding in
a grounding agent now requires the retriev ed knowledge context to be passed to both the
RAG and guardr ail agent. The RAG agent uses the context to gener ate an answer , and the
grounding agent to confirm that the answer is grounded in the context.
Figure 7.5 RAG knowledge agent coupled with a grounding agent. Both agents are provided with the same
context. The RAG agent generates an answer from the context, and the grounding agent verifies that the
answer is grounded by reviewing the same context.232
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 239 ---
After the answer routes  through the groundin g agent, a few options are possible: appro ve
or reject the answer back to the agent with feedback, essentially acting as a critic; appro ve
or block the answer and output some static answer; pass on the grounding results as part
of the final answer . Determining how best to handle grounding output will depend on your
particular use case, and you could also comb ine options. For example, have a grounding
agent that can reject an answer three times before entirely blocking the output and
providing a static statement.

Listing 7.7 showcases the grounding agent we will use to provide feedback on how well an
answer is grounded. At the top of the listing, a new tool has been added to allow the agent
to retriev e the last context used to answer a question. Below is the strongly typed
grounded answer , and after that, the agent definition. When building a grounding agent, it
is best to keep it more generic and universal so that it can be reused for other knowledge
agents.233

#A A tool that provides the context to the last search context
#B Strongly typed answer for grounded output
#C Grounding agent instructions for evaluating how well an answer is grounded@function_tool
def get_last_context() -> str:    #A
"""
Retrieve the last context used by the grounding agent.
"""
global _last_context
if not _last_context:
return "No context available."
return _last_context
class GroundedAnswer(BaseModel):    #B
"""Output model for grounding agent."""
is_answer_grounded: bool
feedback: str
grounding_agent = Agent(
name="Grounding Agent",

Your task is to evaluate the correctness of answers
based on the provided question, context used,
and output answer.
""",

tools=[get_last_context],    #A

7.3.3 Implementing grounding agents as guardrails
Gener ally, when emplo ying a grounding agent , you want ungrounded output to be blocked

guardr ail and enforce both patterns.

Listing 7.9 06_RAG_grounding_guardrail_agent.py (guardrail)
#A Use the guardrail decorator to make a function into a guardrail
#B Run the grounding agent within the guardrail
#C Output the results of the grounding check
#D Trigger the guardrail tripwire if the grounding has failed
#E Add the guardrail to the agent to monitor output@output_guardrail    #A
async def ground_answer(
context: RunContextWrapper, agent: Agent, output: AnswerResult
) -> GuardrailFunctionOutput:
grounding_input = dict(
question=output.question,
answer=output.answer,
)
result = await Runner.run(    #B
grounding_agent, input=str(grounding_input))
result = result.final_output
return GuardrailFunctionOutput(    #C
output_info={
"answer_is_grounded": result.is_answer_grounded,
"feedback": result.feedback,
},
tripwire_triggered=result.is_answer_grounded is False,    #D
)
agent = Agent(
name="RAG Agent",
instructions="""
You are a retrieval-augmented knowledge agent.
Break down the user's query into smaller parts if needed
to fetch relevant context for the user's query.
""",
output_type=AnswerResult,
tools=[search_knowledge_by_keyword],
model="gpt-4o",  # Specify the model to use
output_guardrails=[ground_answer],    #E
)236

Next, Listing  7.10 shows a main function that runs the agent and now watches for tripped
guardr ail exceptions. Now, we only call the RAG agent to answer the question within a
try/ex cept block. Since the agent has an output guardr ail, we now check for triggered
exceptions, indicating that the guardr ail failed . In this case, the guardr ail is triggered if the
answer is not grounded in the context.
When the guardr ail is triggered, we output a static response, but we could easily have the
RAG agent try again and provide it with feedback captured in the trigger from the
grounding agent. Either way, we now have a RAG agent whose answers are constantly
evaluated to be ground ed in the knowledge context the agent has access to. This can be
critical if you need to be 100% sure that you are not providing invalid, incorrect, or possibly
dangerous answers.
7.3.4 Understanding the role of rubrics in evaluation
Evaluation of agent output is not something we can typically evaluate using a measure of
accur acy or correct percentage. Measuring the performance of an output depends on the
use case and desired outcome. If that is as simple as determining if the response was right
or wrong, it is all the better . Howev er, in most cases, ev aluation won t be that simple.
In education, the concept of a rubric  defines a structured set of criteria and standards
that a student must meet to receiv e a particular grade. A rubric can also be used to
determine a guide for the performance of an agent's output. We can follow these steps to
define a rubric we can use to ev aluate the performance of an output:Listing 7.10 06_RAG_grounding_with_guardrails.py (main function loop)
#A Capture the answer from the answer result
#B If grounding fails output a static answerasync def main():
for benchmark in _benchmarks:
question = benchmark["q"]
try:
result = await Runner.run(agent, input=question)
result = result.final_output
answer = result.answer.strip()    #A
except OutputGuardrailTripwireTriggered as e:
print(f"Guardrail tripped. Info:
{e.guardrail_result.output.output_info}")
answer = "No grounded answer available."    #B
print("" + "=" * 40)
print(f"Question: {question}")
print(f"Answer: {answer}")237
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 244 ---
1. Identify the Purpose and Objectives : Determine the goals y ou want
the output to accomplish. F or example, do y ou want to ev aluate the
qualit y of recommendations for a giv en audience or o verall qualit y for a
given subject, format, or other input?     
2. Define Criteria : Dev elop a set of criteria or dimensions that y ou will use
to evaluate the output. These criteria should align with y our objectiv es
and pro vide clear guidelines for assessment. Each criterion should be
specific and measur able. F or example, y ou ma y want to measure a
recommendation b y how well it fits with the genre, as well as b y subject
and format.     
3. Create a Scale : Establish a r ating scale that describes the lev els of
performance for each criterion. Standard scales include numerical scales
(e.g., 1-5) or descriptiv e scales (e.g. , Excellent, Good, F air, Poor).    
4. Provide Descriptions : For each lev el on the scale, pro vide clear and
concise descriptions that indicate what constitutes a strong performance
and what represents a weak er performance for each criterion.     
5. Apply the Rubric : When assessing a prompt or profile, use the rubric to
evaluate the prompt's performance based on the established criteria.
Assign scores or r atings for each criterion, considering the descriptions
for each lev el.    
6. Calculate the Total Score : Depending on y our rubric, y ou ma y calculate
a total score b y summing up the scores for each criterion or using a
weighted a verage if some criteria are more important than others.     
7. Ensure Evaluation Consistency : If multiple ev aluators are assessing
the profile, it's crucial to ensure consistency in gr ading.     
8. Review, Revise, and Iterate : Periodically review and revise the rubric
to ensure it aligns with y our assessment goals and objectiv es. Adjust as
needed to impro ve its effectiv eness.     
Rubric evalu ation is a concept  that can be applied in a critique or evaluation of output it
defines how well a response is aligned with a given rubric's specific criteria and standards.
You can also think of this as the baseline expectation of an output.
There are several other important consider ations when using grounding with profile
evaluation. The list below highlights some key consider ations for each concept we have
covered:
Rubrics  - Rubric dev elopment and ev aluation refer to aligning responses
with the criteria, objectiv es, and context defined b y the rubric and output.
This itself is a complicated set of steps that ma y require additional
iteration to refine the best rubric for y our specific use case.     
Evaluation  - Evaluating in volves assessing whether the response directly
addresses the rubric criteria, sta ys on topic, and adheres to an y provided
instructions. There are man y ways to ev aluate LLMs, from ha ving them
act as a Judge or ev aluator to deterministic code or a human.     238
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 245 ---
Scoring  - Evaluators and ev aluations gauge accur acy, relev ance, and
adherence to standards when assessing rubrics. When building rubric
evaluation systems, be sure to record and tr ack scores (rubrics) for later
analysis and impro vements.     
Logging  - Evaluating/critiquing ensures that the response output is
firmly rooted in the specified context, making the assessment process
more objectiv e and meaningful. Howev er, we w ant to establish output
tracing not just for the gener ative/answer agents but also the ev aluation
and critic agents to caputure, review or flag incorrect outputs.     
A well-ev aluated response aligns with all the rubric criteria within the given context and
objectiv es. Responses that fail will miss the entire criteria, context, and goals.

--- PAGE 247 ---
A critic agent is a specializ ed evaluator that scores output against the entire rubric (criteria
+ scale). We can build on this example to showcase building a critic agent that evaluates
the image according to the style guidelines rubric. Rating the image for how well it has
applied the styles and passing/failing the image based on these guidelines and the accepted
rubric score.
WHY RUBRICS MATTER
If we allow an LLM/agent evaluation (LLM as a Judge) that just passes or fails an output,
we get very little insight into why it passed or failed. The criteria in a rubric provide a
detailed critique or what was good or bad about an output. In turn, this allows us to refine
and impro ve the bad and still mak e sure the good remains.
Before we look at the code, let's go through the process again of building a rubric to
evaluate a response output. There are eight steps to defining a rubric as applied to our
image gener ation example:
1. Identify the Purpose and Objectives : The purpose of our image-
gener ating agent is to gener ate an image pro vided with a strict set of
guidelines.     
2. Define Criteria : For simplicit y, we will ev aluate how a particular output
image aligns with the st yle guidelines pro vided below and used previously
to gener ate images:     
3. Create a Scale : Again, k eeping things simple, we will use a simple scale
of 1-5 (1 is poor and 5 is ex cellent ).     
4. Provide Descriptions : See the gener al descriptions for the r ating scale
shown below:     ## Style Guidelines for All Images:
- **Consistency**: Each image should maintain the same photographic quality with
3D-rendered elements seamlessly integrated
- **Color Palette**: Use a consistent scheme of blues, purples, warm golds, and
greens with pops of bright accent colors
- **Lighting**: Professional photography lighting with dramatic but warm tones
- **Infographics**: Semi-transparent holographic projections that don't overwhelm
the main subjects
- **Characters**: AI robots should be cute, approachable, and distinctly
different from each other while maintaining a family resemblance
- **Icons**: Use universally recognizable symbols (lightbulbs for ideas, gears
for processing, hearts for alignment, etc.)
- **Mood**: Optimistic, educational, and slightly futuristic without being cold
or intimidating241
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 248 ---
5. Apply the Rubric : With the rubric assigned at this stage, it is an
excellent ex ercise to ev aluate the rubric against gener ated images
manually .    
6. Calculate the Total Score : For our rubric, we will tak e an o verall score
for all criteria but y ou could also score each element and tak e a combined
or average score to ev aluate.     
7. Ensure Evaluation Consistency : Asking the LLM to gr ade and then
pass/fail pro vides more consistent results o ver just asking for a pass/fail.     
8. Review, Revise, and Iterate : We will review , compare, and iter ate on
our profiles, rubrics, and the ev aluations themselv es.    
This basic rubric can now be applied to evaluate the responses for our image gener ator. We
will create a critic agent that follows and grades the images according to our rubric and
then pass fail the image based on our criterion score, which is two out of five based on
point 4 abo ve.
Listing 7.12 adds the critic agent along with a vision tool (describe_image , not shown)
and the means to evaluate the styles using our gener ated rubric. This agent along with the
image generation agent now run in a loop controlled by the critic passing or failing
gener ated images style. The loop will continue to run as long as the critic fails the output
image.Score the image on a scale of 1 to 5 using the following scale:
1. **Poor**: The image does not meet the style guidelines at all.
2. **Fair**: The image meets some of the style guidelines but has significant
issues.
3. **Good**: The image meets most of the style guidelines but has minor issues.
4. **Very Good**: The image meets all the style guidelines with only a few minor
issues.
5. **Excellent**: The image meets all the style guidelines perfectly.
Pass the image if it scores 2 or higher, otherwise fail it.242

critic,
f"Please critique the image at {image_path} with the prompt:
{image_description}",
)
critique = critique_result.final_output
failing = critique.image_pass is False    #E
feedback = critique.feedback    #F243
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 250 ---
According to our rubric, defined in point 4 previously , we only require a score of two out of
five or higher to pass. Since this is a relativ ely low score, we should expect the critic to
pass images easily . If we increased the minimum score to a 3 or 4, we would certainly
expect the critic to fail most images, and the loop ma y take a long time to run.
The same principles we used to build the critic agent here can be applied to other use
cases where the output  is gener ated and subject to high variabilit y. Even with such output
variation, we can estab lish rubrics to allow critical agents to evaluate complex outputs such
as images, reports, gr aphs, and other more complicated examples.
7.4 Phoenix for evaluation and feedback
The default OpenAI Dashboard Traces page can be beneficial, but as your agent
development and systems mature, you require more functionalit y. Arize Phoenix is an
excellent open-source platform that can be accessed over the cloud or locally in a Docker
container . You can also self-host a server that can provide access to others, host projects,
track token usage and costs, tool usage, latency, user sessions, metrics, and of course,
agents. It can also be invaluable for diagnos ing complex bugs within an agent process.
Without trace-lev el observ ability, its almost impossible to debug or impro ve complex agent
flows at scale
On top of all that, it even provides mechanisms to track and evaluate agent prompts,
models, and other factors. It also offers quick connectivit y for most agent platforms,
including OpenAI Agents SDK. Figure 7.6 provides an overview of Arize Phoenix, showing
the various tr acing, tr acking, and ev aluations that can be performed.244
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 251 ---
Figure 7.6 Arize Phoenix deployment patterns and use case methods of tracing, tracking and evaluating
agents.
Phoenix provides the ability to trace and track usage across all your agents as well as
capture and record agent activit y. After capturing activit y, you can then quickly and easily
create experiments and evaluators based on metadata and annotations or customiz ed to
your use case. It is highly recommended that if you are a serious agent engineer , you
should ha ve Phoenix in y our set of tr acing, ev aluation, and dev elopment tools.
7.4.1 Connecting to Phoenix
Phoenix uses the underlying Open Telemetry tracing mechanism that OpenAI Agents and
other agent platforms use by default. As such, getting set up is typically quick but not
necessarily always painless. In most cases, you may encounter documentation challenges
or package conflicts, but fortunately , GPT-5 or other top-tier models can help resolv e these
issues.245

7.6 Summary
Robust agents require ev aluation and feedback bak ed in from the start 
tie the Learn step of Sense-Plan- Act-Learn to external ev aluators, a
feedback store, and (optionally) a monitoring/alerting feedback agent.     
Use a mix of methods: red-team testing for safet y breaks, benchmark
testing for goal attainment, human feedback (thumbs/comments) with
verification to reduce bias, and automated ev aluators
(grounding/critic/ev aluation agents) where appropriate.     
Test-Driven Agent Dev elopment (TDAD) flips the process: define
benchmarks (incl. wrong examples), expect initial failures, run each test
multiple times for consistency , then mak e the minimum
prompt/tool/model changes needed to passretest and refactor
iteratively.    
When benchmarks conflict, decide explicitly: accept partial pass targets,
split into specializ ed agents (plus triage), or refactor the agent s
instructions from first principles.     
Prefer tool guidance in the tool s name/docstring o ver prompt clutter 
rename tools and write clear docstrings; k eep prompts lean and role-
focused.     
Add ev aluation agents to score complex, natur al-language outputs with
typed results (e.g. , pass/fail + feedback), a voiding brittle string matching
and enabling progr ammatic gating.     
Grounding agents are essential for RAG verify answers are supported b y
provided context/citations; on failure, regener ate, block with a static
response, or pass grounded feedback forw ard.    
Guardr ails oper ationaliz e grounding wrap agents with output guardr ails
that trip on ungrounded answers and surface feedback/flags for policy
(retry , block, or annotate).     
Rubrics turn subjectiv e qualit y into criteria and scalesuse them with
critic agents for high- variance gener ation (images, reports, gr aphs); loop
gener ation   critique until a passing score or attempt limit.     
Because LLMs are stochastic, benchmark for stabilit ynot just accur acy
by rerunning tests and w atching v ariance, latency , and tok en use.     
Observ ability is non-negotiable: OpenAI T races show ev ery tool call and
LLM turn; Ariz e Phoenix (OpenT elemetry) adds projects, sessions,
metrics, costs, experiments, and ev aluators.     254
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 261 ---
Instrument Phoenix cleanly set the collector endpoint, replace default
processors, register a tr acer, and wr ap runs with named tr aces; attach
sessions and metadata for filtering and cohort analysis.     
Build datasets from spans, run experiments/ev aluators, and use
annotations to capture structured human feedback feed these signals
back into prompts, tools, and policies.     
Production rule: nev er promote agents without activ e evaluation/feedback
loopsbenchmarks, grounding/guardr ails, critics with rubrics, tr acing,
and monitored KPIs (latency , cost, qualit y) working together .    255

8  Deploying agents
and agentic syst ems
This chapter covers
Strat egies f or consuming and deplo ying agents 
Doc kerizing agent syst ems 
Considering adv anced deplo yment strat egies 
Security , saf ety & go vernance in pr oduction 
At some point in your agent journey , you will find the need to deplo y and perhaps
productiz e your agents . This could be as simple as embedding an agent within an existing
application or something more scalable and controlled, like containers. There are numerous
strategies, pr actices, and implementations for building AI agent systems.
In this chapter , we will look at how agents built with OpenAI Agents SDK may be
deplo yed and productiz ed. Keep in mind that there are numerous agent frameworks and
platforms you can develop and deplo y your agents with, and the skills covered in this book
will apply to all of them. By the end you should be able to choose an appropriate
deplo yment strategy for your agent, contai nerize it, orchestr ate multiple agents, and
understand the basics of securing and oper ating them in production.
8.1 Strategies for consuming agents
For much of this book, we have looked at simple toy examples that put into practice agent
concepts. However, most of these examples omitted how such agents could be consumed
or deplo yed. How we consume agents may often dictate how we deplo y those agents, be
they embedded in an application or deplo yed as a separ ate microservice.256
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 263 ---
Figure 8.1 shows three common and simple patterns for consuming and deplo ying
agents. Consumption may come in the form of being embedded in the same application
code, hosted as a microservice and accessible through an API, or more sophisticated and
either consumed as a tool b y other agents o ver MCP or through A2A.
Figure 8.1 shows three simple patterns for deploying and consuming agents. From embedded agents, a
microservice API is accessible or used as a tool through other agents.257
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 264 ---
The simplest method to consume and deplo y an agent is by making it a part of your same
code base. For a web application, this means running the agent directly in the browser on a
user's computer . Since the heavy lifting is done by the LLM and with tools delivered by MCP,
this can be an effectiv e method for consumin g/deplo ying agents. Howev er, the practice of
embedding agents is only recommended for applications that require responsiv e interaction
with an agent. Long-running or multiple- agent systems requiring a strong separ ation of
concerns are not well-suited to this method. Table 8.1 highlights each of the patterns from
the figure breakdown the strengths of each.
Table 8.1 Evaluating agentic deployment patterns

Agents are self -containedNo front end
In software development, a key principle is separ ating code into strongly related concerns.
This, in turn, allows for easy upgrades and component swaps later. A modern approach to
building multiple connected components is called microservices, where each code
component (concern) is hosted as a service within a container .
MICR OSER VICES CONT AINER AR CHITECTURE
Containerization is the process of putting application code into a mini-hosted
operating system called  a container . Containe rs are run within a process stack that
allows you to manage,  control, and deplo y containers. Popular container stacks are
Docker Desktop , Kubernetes, and OpenShift.
When we slice up code into small services and then host and deplo y them as
containers, we call this a microservice. Benefits of doing this include easier code
swaps or upgrades, increased scalabilit y, impro ved securit y, and accessibilit y. Agents
make for prime candidates for containerization because they are often well isolated
and self -contained.258
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 265 ---
We will look at all three  agents' consumption/deplo yment strategies: application embedded,
microservice through API, and microservices  as a tool or through A2A throughout this
chapter . Exploring and running each of the examples on your own can help enlighten you to
the appropriate use case and bey ond.
8.1.1 Embedding real-time voice agents into web applications
Responsiv e real-time voice agents are well-suited to being embedded directly into the
client -side JavaScript of a browser application. Because these interactions are in real-time,
we want the agent system optimiz ed to respond quickly to user changes and therefore
dont want high latency . This typically means keeping the agents' tools optimiz ed and not
relying on heavy backend processing. Remember that this is demo code; in production, we
would use ephemer al keys minted via server-side auth. Later in this chapter , we will look at
a more production example.
The OpenAI Agents SDK for Python is an excellent platform for learning about and
building agents. There are, of course, plenty of other platforms to explore, but the OpenAI
Agents platform offers the ability to make agents with JavaScript. This means if you want to
develop web-based agents, you can quickly switch to JS and build away using the same
patterns and principles.
CODE GENERA TION (VIBE CODING)
Vibe coding, coding using an LLM or agent, is a great way to build examples and
learn about how things work. The samples in this book have been a combination of
vibe coding and agent gener ation. Due to the inherent complexit y of the samples in
this chapter , all examples were gener ated using an LLM. For this reason, we will stick
to just the highlights in the examples. If you feel the need to understand the code in
more detail , a modern LLM like GPT-5.2 or Gemini will surely be able to assist you
further . Remember , vibe/agent coding is a powerful tool that can be both good and
bad. Good because it can quickly scaffold full working examples, and bad because
not all engin eering concerns may be well addressed. Always strongly consider the full
engineering concerns of y our code no matter where it comes from.

8.1.2 Hosting agents through an API
Next, we will look at hosting an agent in a web service and providing access (consuming)
through an API. This example uses FastAPI to wrap the agent code and allow clients to send
messages over HTTP. Listing 8.2 shows snippe ts of the code that handles a post request for
the client. This is a pattern that could be extended to wrap any agent or tool into a post
endpoint and return a simple clean pa yload.
The underly ing agent itself is the image generation agent we built from chapter 7 that
demonstr ates the agent -critic pattern (07_image_gener ation_agent.p y). All that code
remains the same, and the listing only shows the API code. The agent code that uses the
image gener ation tool is not shown.
Listing 8.2 02_app.py (API code only)
#A Create the app that will host the API
#B Create a typed data class to constrain the input into the API
#C Wrap a app.post decorator around the function that consumes the input request
#D Images are handled as base64 strings, extract the image into a base64 string
#E Return the image as an array of bytes but the base64 image could also be returnedapp = FastAPI(
title="Fixed-Config Image Generator API", 
version="1.0.0")    #A
class GenerateIn(BaseModel):    
input: str = Field(..., 
description="Plain-language request (the agent crafts the prompt)."
)    #B
@app.post("/generate", response_class=Response)    #C
async def generate(body: GenerateIn):
agent = build_agent()
with trace("Image generation"):
result = await Runner.run(agent, body.input)
b64 = extract_image_b64(result)    #D
if not b64:
raise HTTPException(
status_code=500, detail="Image generation tool produced no output."
)
png_bytes = base64.b64decode(b64)    
return Response(content=png_bytes, media_type="image/png")    #E262
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 269 ---
The code in the listing is standard for building an API with FastAPI. We use a POST method
because the request is long-running and the text description could be longer than a GET
would support. Returned from the gener ate endpoint is an image as an array of bytes,
making it easy to handle. We could likewise return the base64 string of the image, but the
client would ha ve to decode the string.

configur ation provided for VS Code. The comm and to run the code from the root folder is
shown below:
After the API is running, you can call it using the cURL or PowerShell commands shown
below:

8.2 Dockerizing agent systems
Deplo ying Python web API applications, such as those we developed earlier , can be
relativ ely simple using various cloud platform s, including Azure and Amaz on. Howev er, a
better strategy is to containeriz e agent API applications into microservices. Microservices
architecture is a pattern of deplo ying small, focused API applications that other services can
use.
We can easily apply this pattern to multi- agent architectures where each agent is
focused on performing a single task or sub-goal. Not only does this allow us to upgrade and
swap out agents easily , but it also enables us to add or remove agents. Figure 8.4 shows an
example of our browser -based agent using multiple microservice agents as tools.266

8.3 Considering advanced deployment strategies
As we move from a single proof of concept to a dependable, multi- agent system,
deplo yment choices start to matter as much as prompts and tools. This section distills
patterns that work in practice for choosing where the agent runs, how agents communicate,
maintaining state, and rolling out changes without surprising users (or y our w allet).
8.3.1 Choosing a runtime: edge, API, or event-driven
Where the agent lives dictates latency , cost, and complexit y. Figure 8.8 provides a decision
flowchart that helps determine where agents should exist within our deplo yment.276
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 283 ---
Figure 8.8 a helpful decision flowchart for deciding agent deployments.
As the figure shows, agent latency is a key indicator of where and how to deplo y your
agents, typically dictated by the desired user experience. If you want your agent to be
more interactive and attentiv e, you usually prefer low latency . Conversely , agents with little
user inter action can ha ve much higher latency .
For example, a custome r support agent, directly interacting with users requires very low
latency (real-time) and would benefit from being real-time. While an autonomous research
agent, that spends minutes or hours performing tasks would well suited to a worker/queue
deplo yment infr astructure.
Based on your determination regarding the required latency , we can proceed to selecting
the best deplo yment strategy . Below are the primary deplo yment strategies that are
commonly considered in more detail:277
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 284 ---
Edge (browser/mobile): The agent logic ex ecutes client -side and
streams to a real-time model. Best for con versational/v oice UI with v ery
low perceiv ed latency . Keep tools simple and stateless or pro xy them
through y our back end. Use short -lived client secrets minted serv er-side.     
Synchronous API microservice : Wrap an agent in a web service (e.g. ,
FastAPI) and call it from apps or other agents. Suitable for
request/response inter actions, such as image gener ation, formatting,
lookup , and summarization.     
Event-driven worker agents: Send long or burst y tasks to a queue and
let a work er agent complete them asynchronously . Ideal for jobs that
exceed HT TP timeouts, require retries, or benefit from concurrency
control.     
Typically , you will want to pick the simplest runtime that meets your latency target and tool
requirements. If in doubt, start with API, move hot paths to edge (browser), and offload
heavy tools to event-driven worker agents. As a guide, if you need low-latency
conversational UX then go for edge. If you have normal request/response workloads, then
API. If tasks are long-running or burst y, then ev ent-driven work ers
8.3.2 The three wires of communication
How we communicate to agents and tools will be dictated by required latency and typically
we consider connections o ver three pr actical wires,  each with their own tr ade-offs:
WebRTC/WebSocket (Realtime): Full-duplex audio and text with
minimal o verhead. Supports barge-in (interruptible speech), streaming
TTS, and tok en-by-token responses. Ideal for v oice UX and inter active
canvases. An example of this form is the real-time browser v oice agent
developed earlier .    
HTTP + SSE (MCP): Simple request/response with streaming output.
Easy to pro xy, log, and cache. Ideal for tool calls from agents and for web
clients that w ant streamed text. This example maps well to our earlier
FastAPI example.     
Message bus: Queues (e.g. , Redis/NA TS/Kafka) for decoupled tool
execution. Let the user continue talking while a slow tool runs in the
background, with the result posted back to the session when ready .    
In most cases, the wire (communication channel) you use will be dictated by your latency
and deployment requirements. For example, you may choose to connect to MCP servers
locally using STDIO instead of HT TP+S SE.278
 Manning Publications Co. To comment go to liveBook


--- PAGE 285 ---
